# chapter 2A Study to Discuss the Potential of Mobile Network Optimization Using Machine Learning

Geeta Arora, Jaya Gupta, and Shubham Mishra

DOI: [10.1201/9781003472506-2](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003472506-2)

## 2.1 Introduction

Proactive networks are the cornerstone of future cyber-physical-social systems (CPSS). They offer a unique combination of services: high-speed broadband, ultra-reliable low-latency communication, and massive machine communication, making them ideal for connecting everything in the future of cyber physical social system landscape. 5G and its successors must achieve a staggering 600× to 2,500× increase in capacity, sub-1 mm latency, and support for a mind-boggling 10,000 or more low-rate devices per cell site \[[1](#ch10.xhtml#r123),[2](#ch10.xhtml#r124)\]. While significant improvements boost 5G network quality, they also come with a steep increase in operational costs (OPEX) and optimization complexity, which could be nearly 60 times higher according to a study \[[3](#ch10.xhtml#r125)\]. This poses a serious challenge, as some mobile operators might end up spending more than they earn by the end of the decade if left unchecked \[[4](#ch10.xhtml#r126)\]. As a result, there are concerns that the high costs associated with maintaining the ambitious quality of 5G heterogeneous networks could significantly outweigh the benefits.

The exponential demands of 5G threaten to be outpaced by soaring operational costs (OPEX) and optimization complexity. However, proactive network optimization offers a transformative solution and emphasizes the potential for a major shift in how performance and cost are traditionally balanced. One successful example within 3GPP (the 3rd Generation Partnership Project) emphasizes the intelligent management of video content delivery and the use of attention information to gauge how interested users are in something – a promising implementation in CPSS that integrates cloud-edge computing techniques \[[5](#ch10.xhtml#r127)\]. Data-driven proactive optimization bridges the gap between network resources and predicted user behavior. This allows for anticipating and preventing negative experiences like signal outages or congestion before they occur. For instance, 3GPP Release 15 incorporates user mobility patterns into the Access and Mobility Management Function, enabling prioritized radio resource management \[[6](#ch10.xhtml#r128)\].

Proactive network optimization in 5G promises a revolution, but challenges remain. Current efforts struggle to gather high-resolution consumer data while accounting for uncertainty across network slices. While online information offers a potential solution, its accuracy and reliability are limited by platform bias and sparsity. The chapter explores the potential of dealing big data analytics of online information with machine learning for proactive network optimization. The study aims to identify demonstrably useful applications and define the boundaries of this approach’s effectiveness. The shift from passive/reactive to proactive optimization necessitates the introduction of entirely new network elements, unseen in previous generations (1G–4G) or current 5G standards.

### 2.1.1 From Reactive to Proactive Network Optimization

The study focuses on radio resource management (RRM) and network deployment, encompassing key optimization functions like scheduling, caching, and interference management. Historically, RRM relied heavily on engineering expertise, with manual configuration dominating the late 1990s. The approach then shifted toward reactive numerical optimization post-2010, incorporating expert-driven functions with parameter inference. To understand the cutting-edge world of proactive optimization, let’s start with a quick look back at how optimization has evolved throughout history.

#### 2.1.1.1 Proactive Network Performance Management

Earlier, 2G networks relied heavily on radio engineers who monitored statistics and manually adjusted settings to improve performance metrics (key performance indicators, KPIs). Their decisions were based on field knowledge and experience gained from activities like drive testing \[[7](#ch10.xhtml#r129)\]. However, this manual approach could take hours to diagnose and fix problems, leading to lengthy network recovery times. 3G ushered in the era of automation to reduce human intervention. Researchers and operators explored solutions like 3GPP’s minimization of drive testing (MDT) \[[8](#ch10.xhtml#r130)\] and Markov decision tree-based optimization for traffic offloading in WCDMA networks \[[9](#ch10.xhtml#r131)\]. These advancements aimed to reduce reliance on engineers but still required frequent configuration adjustments and weren’t personalized to individual users. Optimizations were typically targeted toward broader service areas or service types, like maximizing speed in a city or ensuring fairness across users. Additionally, even these automated schedulers still exhibited significant delays in network recovery, taking over an hour in some cases \[[10](#ch10.xhtml#r132)\].

#### 2.1.1.2 The Evolving Landscape of Self-Optimization

The era of 4G saw a significant shift with the introduction of self-organizing networks (SON) in 3GPP Release 8 \[[11](#ch10.xhtml#r133)\]. SON aimed to automate network optimization, enabling cell sites to manage their own coverage, capacity, energy usage, and load balancing \[[12](#ch10.xhtml#r134),[13](#ch10.xhtml#r135),[14](#ch10.xhtml#r136),[15](#ch10.xhtml#r137)\]. Techniques like reinforcement learning and fuzzy controllers were employed \[[12](#ch10.xhtml#r134),[15](#ch10.xhtml#r137)\]. However, a key challenge emerged: the data used by these machine learning approaches often lacked crucial dimensions. Limited information like channel estimates and quality of service reports (QoS) restricted personalization and proactive optimization capabilities \[[14](#ch10.xhtml#r136)\]. As a result, advanced SON engines typically took over 10 minutes to react to network issues \[[11](#ch10.xhtml#r133),[14](#ch10.xhtml#r136)\].

#### 2.1.1.3 Challenges and Solutions for Data Usage Gaps

Recent researches in RRM and network optimization rely heavily on mobile network data (subscriber and cell-level) as the sole source \[[16](#ch10.xhtml#r138),[17](#ch10.xhtml#r139),[18](#ch10.xhtml#r140),[19](#ch10.xhtml#r141)\]. However, this approach has limitations. While general user behavior is crucial, simplifying it into mathematical models and statistical assumptions offer valuable insights, they may struggle to encompass the full complexity of real-world user needs. Most of the techniques resort to simulating a single user-side measurement, neglecting the rich tapestry of user behavior \[[3](#ch10.xhtml#r125),[18](#ch10.xhtml#r140),[20](#ch10.xhtml#r142)\]. The biggest hurdle lies in gathering and combining multifaceted data from diverse sources, particularly when structured and unstructured formats coexist. This lack of a comprehensive consumer picture creates a “cold start” problem, hindering algorithms that need substantial data for initial optimization.

### 2.1.2 Unlocking Proactive Optimization with Essential Tools and Technologies

Mobile Data Applications Impacts of 3GPP’s report highlights a critical factor for improved network optimization: understanding user behavior and traffic patterns through application data \[[21](#ch10.xhtml#r143)\]. To achieve this, optimization needs to consider the “context” of users and applications – essentially, the status information of real-world users (UEs) and software applications \[[22](#ch10.xhtml#r144),[23](#ch10.xhtml#r145)\]. This context encompasses user location, behavior, and characteristics of network traffic, all crucial for a user-centric network. Techniques for gathering this context are referred to as “context-aware” or “context-awareness” approaches \[[22](#ch10.xhtml#r144),[23](#ch10.xhtml#r145)\]. Leveraging cloud-fog-edge computing is essential for processing this context data effectively. To optimize effectively, the algorithm leverages a context-aware module that seamlessly harvests and interprets data from a wide range of sources and personal devices encompassing online data \[[11](#ch10.xhtml#r133)\]. This rich context then empowers the algorithm to intelligently reallocate communication resources, optimizing network performance for individual users and applications. Some of the essential components are described below.

#### 2.1.2.1 Online Data

Social media platforms are a treasure trove of user-generated data – a rich tapestry of personal information intertwined with location, public updates, and other details \[[24](#ch10.xhtml#r146)\]. This data offers a direct window into user intent, making it an ideal source for transforming network optimization into an endeavor which is user-centric and proactive. As highlighted in the research, CPSS aim to deliver high-quality services as well as proactive and personalized experiences \[[25](#ch10.xhtml#r147),[26](#ch10.xhtml#r148)\]. Here’s where big data analysis of online information plays an irreplaceable role. By mining social network data, we can gain valuable insights into user behavior and preferences, enabling networks to anticipate and cater to individual needs. Within the vast realm of online data, this survey delves into three key sources with immense potential for network optimization: social networks such as Instagram, Twitter, and Facebook; reviews and comments of the user (Amazon reviews); and like YouTube some multimedia hosting sites. These platforms offer a rich tapestry of user-generated content, brimming with hidden insights about user behavior and preferences. However, it’s important to acknowledge that each data source has its own strengths and weaknesses (merits and pitfalls) that need careful consideration.

#### 2.1.2.2 Social Network Data

The rise of social media has transformed users from passive consumers to active creators and sharers of information. This rich ecosystem provides not only a wealth of public and personal data but also real-time insights for anticipating future network demands \[[11](#ch10.xhtml#r133)\]. Social network data comes in four primary formats: photos/videos, timeline, text, and geolocation. Text and multimedia content offer a vast amount of information about user preferences, but it can be noisy and unstructured. On the other hand, timeline and geolocation data are well-structured but provide a more limited view of user intent. Social networks also capture social connections, which are invaluable for optimizing device-to-device (D2D) communication. Studies like \[[27](#ch10.xhtml#r149),[28](#ch10.xhtml#r150)\] have explored forecasting social ties to enable D2D caching. This involves identifying influential users for content delivery \[[27](#ch10.xhtml#r149)\] and facilitating content sharing within friend circles \[[28](#ch10.xhtml#r150)\].

### 2.1.3 A Data-Driven Framework for Proactive Improvement

Complex computations in our framework rely on a distributed cloud-edge computing architecture, similar to the ones proposed in \[[25](#ch10.xhtml#r147),[29](#ch10.xhtml#r151),[30](#ch10.xhtml#r152)\]. These frameworks partition data processing tasks between the cloud and the edge based on data size and processing needs. The cloud plane, with its superior processing power and storage capacity, handles large-scale, long-term big data analytics (cloud computing) – the “global big data” mentioned earlier. Meanwhile, the edge plane, positioned closer to network users and devices, tackles smaller-scale, real-time data processing tasks (edge computing) – the “local data” processing. Our work adopts a similar approach, utilizing a remote cloud (data-driven proactive optimization module) and an edge cloud, analogous to the cloud and edge planes, respectively. Following a similar approach, our system leverages a remote cloud for data-driven proactive optimization and an edge cloud, mirroring the structure of cloud and edge computing architectures. The remote cloud is positioned between the gateway and the core network, similar to how caching modules are placed near base stations in the core-to-edge network \[[31](#ch10.xhtml#r153)\]. This distributed approach benefits analysis, learning, and data storage, but it hinges on maintaining acceptably low latency for real-time optimization. The function of the framework is illustrated in the following parts.

#### 2.1.3.1 Data

This crucial step lays the groundwork for effective analysis. It involves data collection, cleaning, and storage. While we focus on these core stages, detailed works further break down the process into data organization, representation, cleaning, reduction, and integration – all well-established practices within CPSS, thanks to the capabilities of edge computing. Data collection involves gathering information relevant to our interests from various offline and online sources, such as government databases, business records, and sensor networks. The data may exhibit variations in completeness and detail across different regions like industrial and urban areas \[[32](#ch10.xhtml#r154)\]. For internet data, providers often offer application programming interfaces (APIs) that allow authorized access to public datasets. Raw data can be noisy and contain redundancies. A cleaning process (e.g., sorting, filtering) is essential to organize it systematically before storing it in edge or cloud data centers for further analysis. Tensor-based methods, which focus on identifying key features, are effective for big data analysis \[[33](#ch10.xhtml#r155)\]. These methods can handle both structured and unstructured data through techniques like extensible order tensors. Studies like \[[34](#ch10.xhtml#r156),[35](#ch10.xhtml#r157)\] showcase how tensors can be leveraged in cloud-edge computing environments. Big streaming data poses another challenge for real-time processing. This technique is valuable for handling the continuous flow of data in real-time optimization scenarios.

#### 2.1.3.2 Predict Consumer Behaviors

Network traffic is a dynamic beast, constantly evolving with user behavior. This step tackles this challenge by building models to forecast these demand fluctuations. These models leverage online data as input, aiming to predict the probability of different traffic demand levels across diverse user contexts and network slices. By describing the “posterior distribution” of these predictions – essentially the range of potential outcomes – can generate a spatial-temporal map of consumer demand. This map serves as a powerful tool for forecasting key network performance indicators (KPIs).

#### 2.1.3.2 Impact on Network KPIs

Correlation models act as a bridge, translating predicted user behaviors into network KPIs, such as network traffic. Polynomial regression and statistical analysis (e.g., Pearson correlation) are two popular approaches for building these models. These models take the probability of different user behaviors (as predicted in the previous step) as input and generate the probability of various KPI outcomes as output. For instance, the model might predict the probability of a “high-load” scenario occurring in the network.

#### 2.1.3.3 Proactive Network Optimization

The magic of proactive optimization lies in using the predicted network KPIs (from the correlation models) to guide network configuration. The optimization algorithm needs to pre-configure network parameters in anticipation of changing conditions to achieve specific performance targets. For instance, a study in \[[36](#ch10.xhtml#r158)\] proposes a framework for proactive and energy-efficient network optimization. Their solution leverages an intelligent platform that sits between the core network and the pool of baseband units (BBUs). The platform optimizes the network by analyzing user behavior and traffic patterns. It’s important to note that both the remote cloud and the edge plane can contribute to these analytics and learnings:

*   **Cloud Computing:** Analyzes large-scale datasets to identify general trends in user behavior, such as city-wide traffic patterns during rush hour. This enables “macroscopic” optimization strategies.
*   **Edge Computing:** Processes personalized contexts using data centers, tensors, and data management and analysis techniques based on edge. This personalized element is crucial for user-centric optimization.

## 2.2 A Multifaceted Approach: Data-Driven Strategies for Network Optimization

Data-driven approaches are revolutionizing network optimization in 5G. Research efforts focus on several key areas:

*   **Big Data Analytics:** Studies like \[[37](#ch10.xhtml#r159)\] explore leveraging big data for 5G network optimization. The authors propose a framework for integrating operator data and showcase optimization use cases.
*    **Self-Organizing Network (SON) Architectures:** Pioneering surveys like \[[38](#ch10.xhtml#r160)\] review cellular SON for beginners, providing an in-depth look at projects, features, standards, and design guidelines.
*   **Machine Learning Applications:** Machine learning is a hot topic in SON. Klaine et al. \[[39](#ch10.xhtml#r161)\] review its applications in this domain, while Chen et al. \[[40](#ch10.xhtml#r162)\] offer a comprehensive tutorial on using artificial neural networks (ANNs) for wireless communication tasks like proactive caching. This study explores various ANN types, including deep neural networks, recurrent, spiking, and feed-forward.
*   **Big Data for Mobility Prediction:** Building on existing big data mobility research \[[41](#ch10.xhtml#r163)\], this study explores methods for geolocation prediction by analyzing user location patterns and mobility trends. They also compare the suitability of data sources like GPS, GSM, and Wi-Fi records for data analysis and model building in this context.

### 2.2.1 Essential Techniques for Data Analysis

These methods can be categorized into two broad areas: data collection and data analysis.

#### 2.2.1.1 Data Collection

To understand and forecast social behavior, we delve into three key categories of online data:

1.  **Social Networks (e.g., Twitter, Facebook):** These platforms offer a treasure trove of user interactions and public information. Geo-tagged social network data is especially valuable because it reveals users’ geographical context, providing a deeper understanding of their activities.
2.  **Content Sharing Platforms (e.g., YouTube, Instagram):** These platforms offer insights into user preferences and content consumption habits through the multimedia content they share. By analyzing the types of videos or photos users engage with, we can glean valuable clues about their interests and behaviors.
3.  **User Reviews and Ratings (e.g., e-Commerce Reviews, Forum Discussions):** These platforms provide a window into user opinions and sentiment. Analyzing reviews and discussions allows us to understand user experiences and preferences related to specific products or topics.

Traditionally, APIs have been the primary method for collecting this data. APIs act as authorized gateways to specific datasets offered by online platforms. By leveraging APIs, we can gather the information necessary for modeling user activities and their associated metadata – a crucial step for social behavior forecasting.

There are three primary methods for collecting online social network data, each with its own advantages and drawbacks:

*   **Application Programming Interfaces (APIs):** This widely used method offers an automated and economical way to collect data directly from service providers. APIs like Search API and Stream API fall under this category. However, limitations include potential inefficiencies, difficulty in acquiring historical data, and data collection limits imposed by service providers.
*   **Building Own Datasets:** This approach offers greater control and potentially more complete datasets. Researchers can collect data from volunteers who agree to participate (e.g., study by Xie et al. \[[42](#ch10.xhtml#r164)\] with 19,484 users) or purchase data from service providers. Incentives like rewards for contribution \[[43](#ch10.xhtml#r165),[44](#ch10.xhtml#r166)\] can boost participation but raise costs and require ethical approval.
*   **Open Datasets:** Leveraging publicly available datasets from organizations like Kaggle \[[45](#ch10.xhtml#r167)\] or the European Union Open Data can significantly reduce collection costs. Examples include the Italia Telecom operator dataset for cellular traffic forecasting \[[46](#ch10.xhtml#r168)\] and datasets from public research projects like DAWN4IoE (cellular traffic data and Twitter density) \[[47](#ch10.xhtml#r169),[48](#ch10.xhtml#r170),[49](#ch10.xhtml#r171)\]. However, researchers need to choose appropriate datasets and pre-process the raw data for further analysis.

#### 2.2.1.2 Data Analysis

The recent rise of scalable machine learning empowers computer systems to learn user behavior from vast online datasets. In the study, there is a discussion of the most commonly used machine learning methods regarding proactive optimization requirements in [Figure 2.1](#ch10.xhtml#f1) and the categories where these methods have been used. Algorithmic complexity helps us estimate the number of computations required to achieve the desired outcome.

![Figure 2.1 Types of machine learning, their applications and level of complexity for desired outcomes.](html/images/9781032751245_C002_001.jpg)

Figure 2.1 Types of machine learning, their applications and level of complexity for desired outcomes.

### 2.2.2 Enhancing Data Utility while Preserving Privacy

Privacy is a paramount concern in data analysis. The challenge lies in maximizing the usefulness (utility) of data while guaranteeing availability, integrity, and confidentiality \[[50](#ch10.xhtml#r172)\]. Ensuring user data security requires a collaborative effort. Network operators and data providers must prioritize both data encryption and strict access controls to prevent unauthorized data activities. Finding the right balance between data utility and privacy requires a three-pronged approach:

1.  **Transparency and User Consent:** Many users are currently unaware of personal data collection, leading to anxieties about potential misuse. Providing clear notices and obtaining informed consent can alleviate these concerns. For instance, the internet of things (IoT) can notify users about data privacy practices (e.g., “right to know”) as mandated by regulations like the EU General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) \[[51](#ch10.xhtml#r173)\]. However, this approach relies on user trust in data storage and usage practices.
2.   **Data Security and Encryption:** Stored data must be protected for both privacy and authenticity. Encryption schemes, such as authenticated encryption \[[52](#ch10.xhtml#r174)\], can transform data into ciphertext using symmetric-key mechanisms to meet these requirements.
3.  **Data Protection and Attack Mitigation:** Data protection goes beyond encryption. Techniques like classifying users based on radio channel information can help identify potentially malicious actors \[[53](#ch10.xhtml#r175)\]. Research in Hamedani et al. \[[54](#ch10.xhtml#r176)\] explores using recurrent neural networks (RNNs) to detect various attack variations, while Atat et al. \[[55](#ch10.xhtml#r177)\] provide a broader survey of security considerations in cyber-physical systems.

### 2.2.3 Modeling Social Behavior

Understanding user behavior and attitudes is crucial for optimizing communication services. People exhibit a wide range of behaviors and opinions toward different communication methods (e.g., video, audio). By analyzing these behaviors, we can choose the most suitable service type for each user described in the following forms.

#### 2.2.3.1 Content Popularity

Content that users frequently access generates a significant portion of network traffic. Understanding content popularity – essentially, what content is most liked, accessed, and shared – is crucial for optimizing network performance.

There are two main goals for modeling content popularity:

1.  **Predicting Popularity:** Forecasting which content will become popular allows for proactive measures to be taken.
2.  **Estimating Popularity Levels:** Accurately gauging the level of popularity helps in making informed decisions about transmission, content placement, and storage within proactive caching systems.

Traditional statistical models (like Zipf distribution) for popularity lack the flexibility of machine learning approaches. Machine learning, leveraging online data, offers a promising path forward for building more accurate popularity models. For instance, regression methods can be applied to analyze the popularity of published YouTube videos, enabling proactive caching decisions for new videos. In the context of caching, even small errors can be costly due to high backhaul costs (error tolerance <0.5% as referenced in Chen et al. \[[56](#ch10.xhtml#r178)\]). Therefore, it’s essential to continuously update these models to minimize overhead and optimize resource allocation \[[57](#ch10.xhtml#r179)\].

#### 2.2.3.2 User Sentiment

User sentiment reflects a user’s perception of specific content or services (positive or negative) and can influence their subsequent actions (e.g., continued use, complaints). Understanding user sentiment is valuable for several reasons:

*   **Improving Quality of Service (QoS):** By aligning services with user preferences, we can enhance the overall user experience (QoS).
*   **Proactive Content Caching:** Identifying preferred content allows for proactive caching, making it readily available to users and reducing network load.

In network optimization, user sentiment can reveal areas with poor performance and content preferences. Social network analysis provides insights into user sentiment, as users often express their experiences (positive or negative) on these platforms. Techniques like sentiment classification can be applied to categorize user complaints on social media according to service type. For instance, a study achieved an F1 score of over 0.6 for blackspot detection in network coverage using Twitter data (as a source for spatial user sentiment analysis) – a case study of London Bridge is presented. Similarly, user requests for specific content reflect their preferences. Studies like \[[56](#ch10.xhtml#r178)\] explore caching user-preferred content at base stations along their anticipated trajectories.

#### 2.2.3.3 User Relationship

Online platforms like Facebook and Twitter capture the relationships between users through connections like friendships and subscriptions. These relationships can be modeled as graphs, with different types including friendship graphs, interaction graphs, latent graphs, and following graphs. Analyzing these social connections offers valuable insights beyond understanding a user’s social role. They also provide a window into information dissemination patterns, which can be harnessed to optimize content delivery \[[58](#ch10.xhtml#r180)\]. Studies like \[[58](#ch10.xhtml#r180),[59](#ch10.xhtml#r181)\] suggest that the strength of social ties influences how information spreads within social-physical networks. In the context of network optimization, leveraging social connections can significantly reduce pressure on the core network. One approach involves strategically sharing cached content within friend circles, identified by analyzing social ties \[[27](#ch10.xhtml#r149)\]. For instance, a study by Zhu et al. \[[28](#ch10.xhtml#r150)\] explores using Facebook friendships for cooperative caching.

### 2.2.3 Using Cellular and Online Information Process of Data-Driven Proactive Network Optimization

The power of online data analysis lies in its ability to reveal hidden patterns and predict user behavior. These insights can be harnessed to forecast cellular traffic patterns, paving the way for a paradigm shift from reactive to proactive network optimization. This section explores how to translate these analytical capabilities into practical implementation strategies for achieving proactive optimization \[[60](#ch10.xhtml#r182)\].

#### 2.2.3.1 Cellular Traffic Prediction

Accurate network traffic prediction is crucial for proactive network optimization, particularly in proactive load balancing. Traditional methods rely on one-step prediction models built from historical network records, focusing on core network or cell-level predictions. However, these approaches are limited in their ability to predict traffic at finer granularities. A promising solution involves analyzing high-resolution GPS data from various sources (heterogeneous datasets) and correlating it with cellular traffic patterns \[[61](#ch10.xhtml#r183)\]. Research in Yang et al. \[[48](#ch10.xhtml#r170)\] confirms a positive correlation between network traffic, online data volume, and the number of network users. This suggests that online social networks can not only predict flash crowd demands but also offer valuable insights for traffic forecasting and resource allocation decisions by network operators.

Traditional methods for spatial traffic prediction rely on mathematical statistics like Zipf distribution and α-stable distributions. However, these methods lack the flexibility of machine learning approaches. Unlike traditional methods, which use techniques like maximum likelihood estimation (approximating parameters without minimizing gradients), machine learning algorithms like neural networks can leverage parameter optimization techniques like gradient descent. While machine learning offers advantages in prediction accuracy, a current challenge is the lack of inherent uncertainty quantification in the generated predictions. This absence of quantifiable uncertainty makes it difficult to assess potential errors and weigh costs and benefits for future decision-making. Promising solutions to address uncertainty quantification include Gaussian processes and deep Gaussian processes, which can produce predictions as posterior distributions that inherently capture uncertainty.

Random traffic spikes pose a significant challenge for network traffic prediction due to the difficulty of explaining and predicting them without additional information about user behavior. To address this limitation, data analysis is moving beyond cellular network data alone and incorporating heterogeneous data sources like Twitter. Current methods often rely on linear regression to model the positive correlation between cellular traffic and social network activity. While statistical methods can quantify the strength of this correlation, they may not effectively capture the underlying dynamics \[[62](#ch10.xhtml#r184), [63](#ch10.xhtml#r185)\]. A critical challenge lies in predicting these random traffic spikes, which often stem from unforeseen events like protests or non-periodic activities. Anomaly detection techniques offer a promising solution to distinguish between regular patterns and anomalous spikes. These techniques often combine clustering algorithms (e.g., K-means) to differentiate traffic patterns and regression models (e.g., neural networks) to predict traffic volume. However, traditional gradient descent optimization in neural networks can lead to locally optimal solutions, potentially hindering prediction accuracy. Neuro-evolutionary deep learning addresses this challenge by incorporating evolutionary techniques to help neural networks escape local optima and achieve better long-term performance with fine-tuned weights.

### 2.2.4 Proactive Load Balancing

Load balancing is essential for managing network traffic imbalances caused by uneven user demand distribution \[[40](#ch10.xhtml#r162)\]. It aims to optimize network performance by strategically transferring mobile users (UEs) from congested cells to nearby idle cells. This is achieved through techniques like adjusting handover offset values, thresholds, and the number of handovers themselves \[[13](#ch10.xhtml#r135)\].

SON algorithms were developed to automate these adjustments, often using fuzzy logic controllers for handover margin tuning \[[20](#ch10.xhtml#r142)\]. However, these controller-based methods face limitations:

*   **Reactive to Traffic Spikes:** They react to sudden traffic spikes, leading to slow response times and limited adaptability to rapidly changing network loads \[[11](#ch10.xhtml#r133)\].
*   **Potential for Oscillations:** In some cases, controller-based methods can introduce oscillations, causing target cells to become overloaded again.

Proactive load balancing approaches leverage machine learning to overcome these challenges. Machine learning algorithms can analyze network data, predict future traffic patterns \[[64](#ch10.xhtml#r186)\], and proactively adjust key parameters like the call blocking ratio (CBR) to optimize network performance and provides information about the following contents:

1.  Balance load with predictive traffic balance load with call block
2.  Ratio or UE locations
3.  Macro cell offloading
4.  Small cell switch-off
5.  Data-driven user association
6.  Proactive load balancing based on online information

### 2.2.5 Proactive Caching

Mobile edge caching algorithms address network traffic demands by strategically storing relevant data on nearby base stations (BSs) based on predicted user needs. This approach significantly improves network performance by enhancing both throughput (data transfer rate) and latency (response time). To maximize the effectiveness of mobile edge caching, proactive consideration of several factors is crucial:

*   **Content Selection:** Identifying the most popular or frequently requested content to prioritize for caching.
*   **Content Placement:** Deciding which BSs to store specific content on, considering factors like user location and mobility patterns.
*    **Content Delivery:** Optimizing the delivery of cached content to users, minimizing delays and network congestion.
*   **Storage Utilization:** Efficiently managing cache storage capacity at BSs to accommodate the most relevant content.

By proactively addressing these elements, mobile edge caching algorithms can significantly improve user experience by ensuring content is readily available at the network edge, closer to users and address the following features:

1.  Content placement
2.  Content delivery
3.  Storage utilization
4.  Dynamic caching with mobility prediction
5.  Cold start problem
6.  Proactive caching based on online information

## 2.3 Conclusion

The study has explored the cutting-edge advancements in online data analytics that hold immense potential for transforming 5G network optimization from a reactive to a proactive approach. The ever-growing volume of online data offers a rich tapestry of information, encompassing individual user contexts (e.g., personal trajectories, preferences, and relationships) and broader social contexts (e.g., popular regions, content popularity, and network traffic patterns). Leveraging machine learning and environmental data, we can predict traffic patterns for different user groups and locations. These forecasts empower proactive network optimization, allowing us to pre-allocate resources to meet diverse needs and adapt to changing network dynamics. This ultimately results in a superior user experience.

# chapter 3AI in Mobile Network Management and Performance Enhancement

Avani Vyas and Shivani Sharma

DOI: [10.1201/9781003472506-3](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003472506-3)

## 3.1 Introduction

Mobile networks are a telecommunication network of mobile devices such as smartphones, tablets, and other wireless-enabled devices that can connect to a network. Mobile networks are also termed as cellular networks \[[1](#ch11.xhtml#r187)\]. A geographical region is divided into cells. Each cell contains a base station which is a physical structure equipped with antennas and transceivers. A base station provides connectivity to mobile devices present in its cell with the rest of the cellular network. The network size and services provided by mobile devices are growing with the growing wireless communication technology. The various communication services supported by mobile networks include voice calls, text messaging (SMS), multimedia messaging (MMS), and data services like internet access. Mobility of the user devices challenges the quality of service (QoS) provided by communication networks. This chapter discusses the key aspects involved in mobile network management and the use of AI in improving users’ mobile network experience.

### 3.1.1 Mobile Network Management

The key aspects of mobile network management include:

1.  **Monitoring Network:** It involves maintaining network performance parameters and fault tolerance. The network performance matrices of prime importance are latency, packet loss, and bandwidth utilization. Whereas fault tolerance involves detecting and identifying network faults to minimize downtime and service disruptions.
2.  **Traffic Management:** Traffic management handles load scheduling in the mobile network in order to optimize the flow of data to prevent congestion and enhance resource utilization.
3.  **Quality-of-Service (QoS) Management:** The QoS of a mobile network depends on traffic management and network monitoring. A well-distributed network traffic load with optimal resource utilization is expected to improve the network performance parameters such as latency, packet loss, and bandwidth utilization.
4.  **Security Management Services:** In the wide connectivity of a network, it is crucial to implement a security mechanism. Security management in mobile networks involves preventing unauthorized access and controlling access to the network and its resources.

### 3.1.2 Scope of Artificial Intelligence in Mobile Network Management

Artificial intelligence (AI) techniques have made a profound contribution to mobile network management by enhancing the network lifetime, efficiency, and security requirements \[[2](#ch11.xhtml#r188),[3](#ch11.xhtml#r189)\]. AI techniques like predictions, machine learning (ML), deep learning, expert systems, and reinforcement learning can be applied to mobile network management in the following ways:

1.  **Predictive Maintenance:** AI-based data analysis tools can be used for predicting network failures and maintenance of networking devices along with real-time monitoring. Predictions help in implementing proactive maintenance strategies for addressing issues that may lead to service disruptions.
2.  **Dynamic Resource Allocation:** The ML models can be implemented in mobile networks for analyzing traffic patterns and dynamic allocation of network resources such as bandwidth to meet the changing demands of users. Overall, ML algorithms can be designed for intelligent traffic management in mobile networks.
3.  **Designing Self-optimizing Networks:** AI algorithms are used to automatically optimize network parameters such as antenna configurations and power levels for improving overall user experience.
4.  **Improving Network Security Aspects:** AI/ML algorithms are used for detecting unusual traffic patterns and anomaly detection and identifying unauthorized access in the network. Similarly, AI algorithms are used for generating automated responses in case of identification of unauthorized behaviors.
5.  **AI for Green Communication:** Predictive AI is very useful in designing green communication networks. Various network components can be programmed for smart sleep modes so that they can optimize the use of their battery powers resulting in prolonged network services.
6.  **AI for Future Generation Communication:** AI techniques can be leveraged for optimizing beamforming, network slicing, and edge computing.

## 3.2 Traditional Methods of Mobile Network Management

This section discusses the non-AI-based traditional methods designed for mobile network management.

### 3.2.1 Mobile Network Optimization

The objective of mobile network optimization is to enhance network efficiency, maximize resource utilization, reduce congestion, and ultimately improve QoS. The targets are managed by optimizing radio frequency planning, implementing load balancing, and employing algorithms to manage traffic and resources more efficiently. Rodriguez et al. \[[4](#ch11.xhtml#r190)\] addressed the problem of increasing network management with the increasing number of mobile users in 5G networks. Challenges majorly include reusing the radio spectrum and handling handovers with the increasing network classifications. The authors used a stochastic geometry approach on radio environment maps to improve the association and coverage probability of the network. The Monte Carlo simulation results show improved coverage probability and handover rates compared to other cell-association techniques. Saad et al. \[[5](#ch11.xhtml#r191)\] also address the same problem of increasing mobile users on the limited availability of cells. The authors investigated trades and tuning of various handover control parameters settings. They concluded that automatic self-optimization is the best for handling such challenges rather than manual handling. Load balancing is a well-explored problem in mobile networks that involves balancing traffic between hot cells (over-loaded cells) and cooler cells (under-loaded cells) \[[6](#ch11.xhtml#r192)–[8](#ch11.xhtml#r194)\]. The traditional methods of load balancing include information-centric networking, cost function-based algorithms \[[9](#ch11.xhtml#r195)\], and parameter-converging algorithms \[[10](#ch11.xhtml#r196)\].

### 3.2.2 Mobile Network Performance Management

The objective of mobile network performance management is to identify the areas of improvement and continuous monitoring of metrics such as data throughput, latency, and network availability. Sherif et al. \[[11](#ch11.xhtml#r197)\] improved the performance of mobile networks by addressing the issue of selfish nodes in opportunistic mobile networks (OMNs). OMNs involve communication between nodes that may not have continuous connectivity. The communication in such networks depends on opportunistic encounters of mobile nodes which sometimes increases communication latency. Therefore, intermediate or relay nodes are used for relaying the messages of disconnected nodes to increase the data throughout and reduce latency. However, sometimes, the intermediate relays may act as selfish and may not cooperate in relaying messages, affecting the network performance and user experience. Sherif et al. \[[11](#ch11.xhtml#r197)\] and Jedari et al. \[[12](#ch11.xhtml#r198)\] used watchdog mechanisms for monitoring and detecting misbehaving nodes in OMNs. Over the years, many such algorithms have been proposed for detecting and handling misbehaving nodes in mobile networks. Kampitaki et al. \[[13](#ch11.xhtml#r199)\] provide a comprehensive survey of such algorithms.

### 3.2.3 Mobile Network Resource Allocation

Mobile devices contain limited energy and computation resources. However, modern applications like real-time tracking systems demand high computation and continuous battery support. In order to support resource-hungry mobile applications, a mechanism of task offloading is used in networks. Task offloading is a process of transferring computational tasks from a mobile device to another computing resource, typically a more powerful server. The results of the computation are then sent back to the mobile device from the server \[[14](#ch11.xhtml#r200)\]. However, transferring computation tasks from mobile devices to servers and back to mobile devices increases communication latency in applications. Radio resource allocation methods are required when multiple mobile devices request task offloading in a cell \[[15](#ch11.xhtml#r201)\]. The solutions proposed include optimal sub-channel allocation \[[15](#ch11.xhtml#r201)\], a heuristic performance-based priority approach \[[14](#ch11.xhtml#r200)\], and multiple parameters that can be combined into non-linear programming problems \[[16](#ch11.xhtml#r202)\].

In conclusion, the traditional methods of mobile network management include designing multi-objective functions and using optimization algorithms like Watchdog and game theory-based approaches for optimizing the performance of the network. However, these methods are difficult to update with the changing network dimensions and users. The modern networks are time-varying and demand self-adaptive solutions for accommodating modern mobile applications.

## 3.3 Artificial Intelligence for Mobile Networks

This section discusses in detail the existing uses of AI in mobile network management.

### 3.3.1 AI-based Mobile Network Optimization

#### 3.3.1.1 Self-healing Networks

Self-healing refers to the ability to detect, diagnose, and mitigate the issue automatically without human intervention. Moysen et al. \[[17](#ch11.xhtml#r203)\] designed a reinforcement learning-based automated self-healing approach for identifying and diagnosing cell outages in long-term evolution (LTE) networks. The AI-enabled base station automatically adjusts the downlink transmission power levels and finds the optimal antenna tilt value for covering the outage zone. Asghar et al. \[[18](#ch11.xhtml#r204)\] used a ML algorithm on network performance matrices to continuously detect anomalies in the network. After detection, fuzzy logic is used to automatically determine the compensation actions. Additionally, a heuristic-based continuous optimization tool is designed for making incremental improvements to the network. Reshmi et al. \[[19](#ch11.xhtml#r205)\] also designed a self-healing framework that uses network performance matrices for detecting anomalies in the network. Riaz et al. \[[20](#ch11.xhtml#r206)\] discussed that the existing efforts in self-healing networks focus on single base stations and single faults. Moreover, they highlighted that the existing AI-based methods fail to perform if dense user datasets are not available during training. In order to overcome the research gap, Riaz et al. \[[20](#ch11.xhtml#r206)\] designed a self-healing framework using CNN and image in painting technique which outperforms many ML models on highly sparse user equipment datasets.

#### 3.3.1.2 Energy Efficiency

Hossain et al. \[[21](#ch11.xhtml#r207)\] forecasted the changing network load and expected energy utilization based on which network slicing decisions are made. They distributed the computation load of ML algorithms among different computation devices available in the network to reduce the energy consumed by different ML algorithms. Peesapati et al. \[[22](#ch11.xhtml#r208)\] addressed the concept of multi-operator radio access network (RAN) sharing, where multiple mobile network operators collaboratively utilize the same physical infrastructure for their radio access networks. This sharing model is aimed at resource efficiency and cost reduction. AI is employed to make intelligent decisions and adjustments to the network configuration with the goal of reducing energy consumption while maintaining performance.

#### 3.3.1.3 Load Balancing

AI has found great importance in load balancing in mobile networks. Gures et al. \[[23](#ch11.xhtml#r209)\] provide a detailed survey of such load-balancing techniques. Alsuhli et al. \[[24](#ch11.xhtml#r210)\] handled the issue of mobility load management in cellular networks. The focus of the research is on dynamically allocating network resources to accommodate the mobility patterns of users. The authors used deep reinforcement learning for dynamically allocating network resources to accommodate the mobility patterns of users. Saad et al. \[[25](#ch11.xhtml#r211)\] highlighted that the cell density is increasing with the increasing user equipment which demands optimized handovers of mobile devices. The authors in \[[26](#ch11.xhtml#r212)\] designed a self-optimizing fuzzy logic-based handover scheme for heterogeneous networks. Three parameters, namely the user speed, SINR value, and load of the cell are input into the designed fuzzy system for estimating the handover control parameters.

### 3.3.2 Intelligent Traffic Management in Mobile Networks

Intelligent traffic management in mobile networks involves the use of advanced technologies and algorithms to optimize the flow of data and resources within the network. The goal is to enhance the overall performance, efficiency, and quality of service in the face of varying network conditions, user demands, and application requirements. Intelligent traffic management encompasses a range of techniques and strategies to dynamically allocate resources, prioritize traffic, and respond to real-time network conditions. Traditional methods of mobile network/internet traffic management exploited queuing theory for handling bottleneck buffer queues in mobile networks \[[27](#ch11.xhtml#r213)\]. With the introduction of AI, researchers have started exploiting AI methods for handling network traffic. Liu et al. \[[28](#ch11.xhtml#r214)\] designed a fuzzy-logic-based controller for automatically measuring router queue size. The designed model helps in taking proactive measures based on measured router queue sizes. Fu et al. \[[29](#ch11.xhtml#r215)\] discussed the services provided with a 5G network along with the potential use of AI for improving network performance. The authors mentioned the potential deployment of distributed ML algorithms at cloud, fog, and edge nodes for 5G networks. Shen et al. \[[30](#ch11.xhtml#r216)\] discussed the issue of QoS in heterogeneous networks. In heterogeneous networks, different types of networks are combined using connecting devices like gateways. Different networks have different traffic rates and service requirements. Shen et al. \[[30](#ch11.xhtml#r216)\] performed classification and management at different layers of convolution neural networks for designing intelligent multi-dimensional heterogeneous traffic control.

AI techniques are also used in prediction-based traffic management \[[31](#ch11.xhtml#r217)\]. Morales et al. \[[32](#ch11.xhtml#r218)\] used artificial neural networks to predict the network traffic and adapt the topology of virtual networks in order to support the increased traffic demand. Virtual networks provide extensions to mobile networks by adding more computation power to the network. Morales et al. \[[32](#ch11.xhtml#r218)\] predicted the maximum bit rate between every origin-destination pair on an hourly basis and adapted the topologies of the virtual network to meet the predicted traffic demand. Similarly, AI techniques are used in optical networks. Optical networks provide backhaul connectivity to mobile networks for transporting mobile network traffic between various network elements, including base stations, core networks, and external data centers \[[33](#ch11.xhtml#r219)–[35](#ch11.xhtml#r221)\]. This ensures efficient and reliable data transport. Leung et al. \[[36](#ch11.xhtml#r222)\] used a single-hidden-layer neural network for estimating the blocking probability of current network traffic conditions in optical networks. In another contribution by Vinchoff et al. \[[37](#ch11.xhtml#r223)\], they exploited graph convolution neural network (GCN) and generative adversarial network (GAN) for predicting the high-traffic situations in optical networks. The study shows that the GCN-GAN model predicts traffic bursts more accurately compared to the traditional LSTM model.

### 3.3.3 Machine Learning for Mobile Network Planning

Mobile network planning involves designing, optimizing, and configuring the elements of a mobile communication network to ensure efficient and reliable service delivery.

#### 3.3.3.1 Network Coverage Planning

Fauzi et al. \[[38](#ch11.xhtml#r224)\] highlighted that the deterministic techniques of network planning are failing to meet the increasing demands of mobile networks. They claimed that ML algorithms are useful in estimating the propagation models over the traditional empirical methods. The authors used a random forest ML algorithm for predicting the reference signal received power and designed an online coverage estimator tool. Dai et al. \[[39](#ch11.xhtml#r225)\] also emphasized using ML algorithms for estimating network coverage over the traditional propagation models as they are complex and time-consuming. They also designed an ML-based network evaluation tool by considering useful parameters such as communication distance, geography, and configuration parameters of the base station. Different ML models are trained on real-world datasets for estimating the received signal strength (RSS). The comparison results on the considered dataset show that SVM achieved better RSS prediction accuracy as compared to the nearest neighbors, decision trees, random forest, and neural network models. Mohammadjafari et al. \[[40](#ch11.xhtml#r226)\] used ML algorithms to estimate the radio coverage and determine optimal radio transmitter locations for a given geographical location. They investigated that creating and adding new features based on the existing features to the ML models further improves the prediction capabilities of models. Mahboubi et al. \[[41](#ch11.xhtml#r227)\] used K-means clustering to increase the area covered by a mobile sensor network. Lyu et al. \[[42](#ch11.xhtml#r228)\] used a supervised learning model for estimating the coverage of mobile networks. They also used AI to generate synthesized datasets and combine them with the real-world dataset. The final dataset is then used for training and testing the supervised learning models. Likewise, over the years, numerous ML techniques have been designed and explored for enhancing cellular networks \[[43](#ch11.xhtml#r229)\].

#### 3.3.3.2 Backhaul Planning

Backhaul is the part of the network that connects the core or backbone network to the small subnetworks at the network edge, typically the access networks. Backhaul must have sufficient capacity to handle the data traffic generated by the connected access points. With the increasing demand for high-speed data services, the capacity of backhaul links becomes crucial for ensuring a responsive and efficient network. Mahmood et al. \[[44](#ch11.xhtml#r230)\] used an ML algorithm for predicting future network demands. The prediction helped in dynamically configuring the wireless backhaul network based on the forecasted traffic. This adaptability allows the network to respond in real time to changing demand patterns and ensures that resources are allocated where they are most needed. Abdelmoaty et al. \[[45](#ch11.xhtml#r231)\] used deep reinforcement learning for designing hierarchical backhaul topology.

## 3.4 Use Cases and Applications

Several telecommunication industries are shifting toward AI-enabled services. As discussed earlier in the chapter, the main goal is to provide automated self-healing and adaptive services to users for handling seamless handovers and network outages. In a recent survey report by Frost and Sullivan \[[46](#ch11.xhtml#r232)\], 71% of the surveyed telecommunication companies experience improved customer services and 63% companies optimized their network management using AI. German ministry funded 5*.*1 million Euros to Ericsson project “AI4Mobile” \[[47](#ch11.xhtml#r233)\]. “AI4Mobile” provides improved results for the prediction of connection quality for Connected Mobility and Industry 4.0. The existing contribution of AI in supporting mobile networks is not limited to this discussion. Digital twins are emerging as the most powerful AI tool that can be used in many ways to improve the services of telecommunications.

### 3.4.1 Digital Twins for Mobile Networks

A digital twin is a virtual representation or digital replica of a physical object, system, or process. It is a concept that combines real-world data with virtual modeling and simulation to create a dynamic, digital counterpart of a physical entity. The goal is to enable monitoring, analysis, and optimization of the physical entity throughout its lifecycle. The digital twin provides a facility for testing a process virtually on a real-life platform without actually causing any physical damage or cost. Sun et al. \[[48](#ch11.xhtml#r234)\] designed a digital-twin network of a mobile network for the fast management of service level agreements (SLA). All the updated network policies are first verified on the digital-twin network for SLA requirements without affecting the physically deployed network services. [Figure 3.1](#ch11.xhtml#f2) shows the digital-twin network adaptation of a real physical network.

![Figure 3.1 Digital twins for mobile networks \[48\].](html/images/9781032751245_C003_001.jpg)

Figure 3.1 Digital twins for mobile networks \[[48](#ch11.xhtml#r234)\].

Deng et al. \[[49](#ch11.xhtml#r235)\] address that the performance of ML algorithms can be improved further by integrating digital-twin networks into them. The current proposed adaptive ML techniques rely on physical networks for training samples and adaptations which causes delays in data collection, training, and deployment. As a solution, the authors considered the digital twin of the current physical network and used expert knowledge to predict the next state of the network. Then, reinforcement learning is used for optimization. The final optimized solution is deployed on the physical network. Likewise, digital twins have found their potential use in designing and testing security and privacy services in mobile networks \[[50](#ch11.xhtml#r236)\]. Ahmadi et al. \[[51](#ch11.xhtml#r237)\] discussed the application of digital twins in 6G networks. They discussed the concept of “networked twins” which refers to a collaborative ecosystem of interconnected digital twins within a network. This includes digital twins of individual devices, components, or even entire systems interacting with each other, sharing data and collectively contributing to the optimization of the entire network.

## 3.5 Emerging Technologies

### 3.5.1 Edge Computing and AI in Mobile Networks

Edge computing involves processing data closer to the source of generation, re-reducing latency, and improving real-time responsiveness. The combination of edge computing and AI facilitates real-time decision-making at the network’s edge. This is particularly valuable for applications requiring immediate responses, such as autonomous vehicles and smart cities. AI algorithms like neural networks demand high processing capabilities. The mobile devices are equipped with limited computing resources. The major processing tasks such as periodic training of adaptive AI models are usually performed at resource-rich cloud networks. However, mobile networks experience communication latency due to the exchange of data and training model parameters between mobile devices and the cloud. Therefore, the major work in the area lies in exploiting distributed edge resources for AI mobile networks to improve response speed. Li et al. \[[52](#ch11.xhtml#r238)\] partitioned the computation of deep neural networks between edge and mobile devices for real-time adaptations with improved user experience. Tran et al. \[[53](#ch11.xhtml#r239)\] presented the idea of upgrading the mobile network infrastructure by integrating mobile edge computing (MEC) capabilities directly at the base stations for further optimizing the services supported by edge networks. The proposed infrastructure was evaluated for three case studies which include a compute-intensive application, pre-processing of large data before sending it to the servers, and context-aware services. As a result, the designed heterogeneous resource pool of MEC and edge proved its potential application in 5G networks.

### 3.5.2 AI for Network Security in Mobile Communication

Security threats in mobile networks pose risks to the confidentiality, integrity, and availability of data and services. As mobile networks evolve, new threats emerge, and existing threats may evolve in sophistication. To name a few, the common security threats in mobile networks include network spoofing, unauthorized and authentication attacks, eavesdropping, and denial of service attacks. The conventional methods of cryptography and security find it challenging to provide services in complex time-varying mobile networks. This provides a scope for artificial intelligence-based methods to address time-varying parameters and provide automated and adaptive security mechanisms. Fang et al. \[[54](#ch11.xhtml#r240)\] explored ML-based authentication methods for 5G networks that exploit physical layer parameters such as carrier frequency offset, channel impulse response, and RSSI values. The pattern recognition algorithms are useful in identifying anomalies in time-series datasets which could lead to security threats in mobile networks. Ali et al. \[[55](#ch11.xhtml#r241)\] identified anomalies due to radio network attenuation and SDN misconfiguration using a clustering algorithm. Jamming attacks are a common threat in mobile networks resulting in denial of service. Krayani et al. \[[56](#ch11.xhtml#r242)\] used a dynamic Bayesian network model to detect jamming in orthogonal frequency division multiplexing subcarriers. Though AI methods are useful in improving the services of mobile networks, they are vulnerable to security attacks. Qiu et al. \[[57](#ch11.xhtml#r243)\] tested a deep neural network model for white-box and black-box attacks and proved that the AI models can be fooled by modifying their parameters or by tampering with the datasets. This demands further actions for addressing vulnerabilities in AI-based security management in mobile networks.

## 3.6 Conclusion

This chapter discusses the use of ML models, predictive analysis, neural network models, and reinforcement learning in enhancing mobile network management services. This chapter provides an overview of the traditional methods of mobile network management followed by a discussion on the latest AI techniques used for designing self-healing mobile networks, automatic load rebalancing, automated network, and backhaul planning. Digital-twin technology has the potential to be transformative for mobile networks, offering substantial positive impacts. With the use of digital twins, tedious real-time tests can be performed with reduced complexities and time.
