# chapter 4Application of Evolutionary Algorithms on Wireless Sensor Network Coverage OptimizationComparative Study

Amir Parnianifard, Abhishek Sharma, and Sushank Chaudhary

DOI: [10.1201/9781003472506-4](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003472506-4)

## 4.1 Introduction

Network coverage, which is essential for collecting location information and delivering perception services, is one of the main issues with wireless sensor networks (WSNs) \[[1](#ch12.xhtml#r244)\]. Sensor networks typically contain a huge number of nodes and operate in complex environments that humans cannot access. Nodes are frequently dispersed at random by aircraft pitching. However, doing so may result in too many or too few nodes in some places, creating blind or perception-overlapping zones. The location of the nodes must thus be adjusted dynamically using an optimization technique \[[2](#ch12.xhtml#r245)\]. In WSN, coverage is frequently used as a metric for service quality. From this vantage point, coverage refers to how effectively the sensors monitor their intended area \[[3](#ch12.xhtml#r246)\]. The literature has given a lot of attention to the WSN coverage optimization problem by maximizing the coverage of the monitoring area \[[1](#ch12.xhtml#r244),[4](#ch12.xhtml#r247)–[6](#ch12.xhtml#r249)\].

Ensuring sufficient coverage while adhering to specific application demands frequently presents NP-hard optimization challenges. These complexities arise due to the need to strategically deploy sensor nodes in a manner that maximizes coverage efficiency while considering constraints such as energy consumption, network connectivity, and environmental factors \[[7](#ch12.xhtml#r250)–[9](#ch12.xhtml#r252)\]. As the number of sensors to be deployed increases, the search space expands exponentially, i.e., if assume *m* represents the number of allowable locations for each sensor, then the search space for *n* sensors can be *m*^(*n*) possible points \[[10](#ch12.xhtml#r253)\]. Many researchers have explored the application of metaheuristic techniques, also known as evolutionary algorithms, to address the coverage optimization problem, achieving significant advancements \[[11](#ch12.xhtml#r254)\].

These methodologies encompass a variety of optimization techniques used in computational intelligence. Genetic algorithm (GA) \[[12](#ch12.xhtml#r255)\] employs principles of natural selection to iteratively evolve solutions through operations like selection, crossover, and mutation, simulating biological evolution. Particle swarm optimization (PSO) \[[13](#ch12.xhtml#r256)\] emulates the collective behavior of birds or fish to iteratively refine solutions based on individual and group experiences within the swarm. Ant colony optimization (ACO) \[[14](#ch12.xhtml#r257)\] draws inspiration from ants’ foraging behavior, utilizing pheromone trails to find optimal paths and solve complex problems efficiently. Ant lion optimization (ALO) \[[15](#ch12.xhtml#r258)\] replicates the predatory strategy of ant lions, leveraging trapping mechanisms to explore and exploit search spaces effectively. Differential evolution (DE) \[[16](#ch12.xhtml#r259)\] is a population-based method that enhances solution quality and convergence speed by iteratively evolving solutions using differences between randomly selected pairs. Finally, the grey wolf optimizer (GWO) \[[17](#ch12.xhtml#r260)\], a newer swarm intelligence algorithm, mimics grey wolves’ hunting tactics and hierarchical social structure to balance exploration and exploitation in search of optimal solutions.

Generally, these algorithms are widely applicable to a range of critical optimization challenges in WSNs. For instance, they are used in localization problems \[[18](#ch12.xhtml#r261)–[21](#ch12.xhtml#r264)\], where the goal is to accurately determine the positions of sensor nodes within the network. They are also employed in designing minimum energy topologies \[[22](#ch12.xhtml#r265)\], which aim to minimize energy consumption while maintaining network connectivity and performance. Another vital application pertains to optimizing the shortest path for data transmission \[[23](#ch12.xhtml#r266),[24](#ch12.xhtml#r267)\]. This optimization objective aims to minimize both the travel costs associated with the sink and the time taken for data collection, thereby significantly enhancing the efficiency and effectiveness of data-gathering processes in WSNs. This optimization task is pivotal for ensuring timely and cost-effective data retrieval across vast sensor network deployments. Additionally, these algorithms are utilized to maximize signal coverage \[[25](#ch12.xhtml#r268),[26](#ch12.xhtml#r269)\], ensuring that the monitoring area is adequately covered by the sensor nodes to detect events and transmit information reliably.

However, this chapter specifically hones in on the common WSN coverage optimization problems \[[1](#ch12.xhtml#r244),[4](#ch12.xhtml#r247)\], addressing the challenge of determining the optimal placement of sensor nodes to maximize the coverage area while minimizing the number of sensors used. This focus is crucial because effective coverage directly impacts the network’s ability to monitor and report on environmental conditions accurately and efficiently. The study explores various metaheuristic algorithms to solve these coverage problems, providing a comprehensive comparison and analysis of their performance in different scenarios.

This study aims to conduct a structured comparative analysis to assess the performance of the four most prevalent metaheuristic techniques—PSO, GWO, GA, and DE—in addressing practical WSN coverage optimization challenges. The study conducts a statistical evaluation of the results generated by these optimizers, focusing on three main criteria:

*   **Accuracy:** This criterion measures how close the optimizer’s solutions are to the best-known or true optimal solution. High accuracy indicates that the optimizer consistently finds solutions that are near the optimal value, demonstrating its effectiveness in solving the given problem.
*   **Robustness:** Robustness assesses the optimizer’s ability to produce consistent results across multiple runs. An optimizer is considered robust if it delivers reliable and stable performance despite variations in initial conditions or stochastic elements in the optimization process. This is typically evaluated using the standard deviation of the fitness values obtained from multiple iterations.
*   **Computational Time:** This metric evaluates the total time required by the optimizer to converge to the optimal solution. It includes the time taken for all iterations and function evaluations. An efficient optimizer should minimize computational time while still providing accurate and robust solutions, making it suitable for real-time or large-scale applications.

Our contributions are twofold: (i) we systematically compare and evaluate these four well-established metaheuristic techniques commonly used in WSN coverage optimization, and (ii) we offer practical guidance to assist practitioners in selecting the most suitable method based on considerations of accuracy, robustness, and efficiency in addressing large-scale coverage optimization problems involving 30–60 design variables associated with sensor set coordinates.

This chapter is structured as follows. Section 4.2 reviews the literature, including recent studies about metaheuristic-based coverage optimization in WSN. The basic methodology of each participated optimizer in the current comparative study is explained in Section 4.3. The statement of the problem behind coverage WSN optimization is mentioned in Section 4.4. Section 4.5 includes the specification of benchmark functions and simulation setup required for systematic comparison of algorithms and implementation of algorithms in benchmark cases. The obtained comparison results are also statistically analyzed and discussed in this section. Finally, this chapter is concluded in Section 4.6.

## 4.2 Related Works

To enhance design quality and reduce the design cycle duration, optimization technologies are increasingly being applied to engineering design challenges. Optimization typically involves identifying the optimal levels of a set of design variables, *X*, to achieve one or more objectives, *f*(*X*), while adhering to constraints on the design variables, *g*(*X*). These constraints, which can be expressed as equalities or inequalities, restrict the design space and complicate the search for the optimal solution. Despite these challenges, the general framework of a mathematical programming model can be described as follows:

  
$$\\begin{array}{l} {\\text{Min}\\,\\text{or}\\,\\text{Max}:\\,\\, f\\,\\left( \\left( ,X, \\right) \\right)} \\\\ {\\,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\text{Subject}\\,\\text{to}:\\,\\, g\_{j}\\left( \\left( ,X, \\right) \\right) \\leq 0,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\, j\\,\\, = \\,\\, 1,\\,\\, 2,\\,\\,\\ldots,\\,\\, J} \\\\ \\end{array}$$  
(4.1) 

The objective function *f*((,*X*,)) and the inequality constraints *g*[*j*](*X*), where *X* represents the decision variables, form the basis of most engineering design problems \[[27](#ch12.xhtml#r270)\]. Since engineering designs typically involve constraints \[[28](#ch12.xhtml#r271)–[30](#ch12.xhtml#r273)\], effective methods are essential to handle these constraints robustly. Optimization approaches that aim to find optimal solutions while satisfying these constraints are broadly categorized into model-based and metamodel-based (or surrogate-based) strategies \[[31](#ch12.xhtml#r274)–[35](#ch12.xhtml#r278)\] and \[[36](#ch12.xhtml#r279)\].

Classical model-based techniques include metaheuristic methods such as GA, PSO, DE, and GWO \[[37](#ch12.xhtml#r280)\]. These methods are favored not only by computer scientists but also by researchers across diverse disciplines due to their straightforward implementation, adaptability, lack of derivative requirements, and capability to escape local optima \[[17](#ch12.xhtml#r260)\].

In scenarios involving “expensive optimization” problems, like those encountered in computer-aided design or simulation-based optimization, researchers often prefer metamodel-based approaches that integrate surrogates with metaheuristics \[[38](#ch12.xhtml#r281)–[43](#ch12.xhtml#r286)\]. These surrogate-based methods allow for efficient exploration of the design space by replacing computationally expensive objective functions with surrogate models, thereby reducing the overall computational burden. The scope of the current chapter is confined to the application of model-based metaheuristic methods, which have shown significant advantages in addressing various WSN optimization problems \[[20](#ch12.xhtml#r263),[44](#ch12.xhtml#r287),[45](#ch12.xhtml#r288)\].

The coverage rate is a fundamental metric in numerous applications of WSNs as it determines the effectiveness and extent of the monitoring area. The deployment strategy of sensor nodes plays a critical role in this regard, as efficient node placement can significantly enhance both energy efficiency and the overall quality of network services \[[46](#ch12.xhtml#r289),[47](#ch12.xhtml#r290)\]. In many scenarios, sensor nodes are distributed randomly within a given workspace, which often leads to an excessive number of nodes. This overabundance can reduce both the coverage and the quality of monitoring due to overlaps and redundancy in the sensor network \[[47](#ch12.xhtml#r290),[48](#ch12.xhtml#r291)\]. Therefore, optimizing the placement of sensors is essential to maximize the coverage ratio of WSNs in practical operational environments.

The challenge of finding the optimal node placement strategy becomes particularly complex when dealing with large-scale sensor networks, where the number of nodes and the area to be covered are substantial \[[49](#ch12.xhtml#r292)\]. In this context, traditional methods may fall short due to their inability to efficiently handle the combinatorial nature of the problem. As a result, researchers have increasingly turned to metaheuristic algorithms to address these optimization challenges, leading to significant advancements in WSN coverage.

Metaheuristic techniques, known for their ability to find near-optimal solutions to complex problems, have been extensively studied and applied in WSNs. For instance, a novel adaptation of the fruit fly optimization algorithm was proposed by Song et al. \[[50](#ch12.xhtml#r293)\], which introduced a new change step specifically aimed at enhancing coverage. This variant showed improved performance in optimizing the placement of sensor nodes compared to traditional methods. Similarly, the ACO algorithm has been applied by Liao et al. \[[51](#ch12.xhtml#r294)\] to expand coverage areas. The ACO algorithm, inspired by the foraging behavior of ants, proved effective in finding optimal paths and positions for sensor nodes, thereby maximizing the coverage.

Another notable contribution is from Ari et al., who utilized the bacterial foraging optimization technique \[[52](#ch12.xhtml#r295)\] to improve coverage rates. This method, inspired by the foraging patterns of bacteria, was successful in optimizing sensor placement by simulating bacterial movement and interaction, which led to better coverage performance. Each of these metaheuristic approaches demonstrates the potential and versatility of such algorithms in solving the complex problem of sensor deployment in WSNs.

Furthermore, the application of these algorithms is not limited to theoretical improvements. Practical implementations have shown that metaheuristic methods can be effectively used in real-world WSN deployments to enhance monitoring capabilities. The continuous development and refinement of these algorithms ensure that WSNs can achieve higher coverage rates, better energy efficiency, and improved service quality, which are critical for various applications such as environmental monitoring, security surveillance, and industrial automation, see \[[1](#ch12.xhtml#r244),[4](#ch12.xhtml#r247)–[6](#ch12.xhtml#r249)\].

Vilovic et al. \[[53](#ch12.xhtml#r296)\] utilized the ACO method to optimize transmitter positions with the goal of maximizing the average received power. This approach involves simulating the behavior of ants to find optimal paths, which in this context translates to finding the best positions for transmitters to ensure maximum signal strength. Comparative studies have shown that while PSO and GA yield similar antenna positions, ACO tends to require a greater amount to fitness function evaluations. This results in slower convergence times for ACO compared to PSO and GA, as detailed in \[[10](#ch12.xhtml#r253),[54](#ch12.xhtml#r297),[55](#ch12.xhtml#r298)\]. Despite this, GA has been notably effective in the design of high-performance antennas, a success attributed to its robust search capabilities and adaptability, which are common traits among most metaheuristic algorithms.

Additionally, PSO has been employed to determine optimal positions for base stations to enhance signal coverage, as noted in Yangyang et al. \[[56](#ch12.xhtml#r299)\]. This method leverages the social behavior of particles, or agents, to iteratively improve the positions of base stations, thereby ensuring optimal coverage with efficient computation. The versatility and effectiveness of PSO make it a popular choice for various optimization problems in wireless communications.

The GWO, introduced by Mirjalili et al. \[[17](#ch12.xhtml#r260)\], has rapidly gained traction in the field of WSNs. GWO simulates the leadership hierarchy and hunting behavior of grey wolves in nature to search for optimal solutions. This algorithm has demonstrated strong capabilities in achieving optimal coverage solutions for WSNs, as supported by the findings by Miao et al. \[[57](#ch12.xhtml#r300)\]. The hierarchical approach of GWO, which involves alpha, beta, and delta wolves guiding the search, has proven effective in balancing exploration and exploitation during the optimization process.

These advancements highlight the significant contributions of metaheuristic algorithms in the field of wireless communications. Each algorithm, with its unique mechanisms and strategies, offers distinct advantages. The choice of algorithm often depends on the specific requirements of the problem at hand, such as the need for faster convergence or higher solution accuracy. As research in this area continues to evolve, these algorithms are expected to become even more refined and capable, further enhancing the performance and reliability of WSNs and other wireless communication systems.

## 4.3 Beyond Metaheuristics Participated in the Current Work

In this section, the basic concepts and terminology of each of the four optimizers which participated in the comparative study in this work, e.g., PSO, GWO, GA, and DE are explained.

### 4.3.1 Particle Swarm Optimization

Reference \[[13](#ch12.xhtml#r256)\] introduced the standard PSO algorithm, which models the collective behavior observed in social animals such as fish schools and bird flocks. This algorithm utilizes a population of particles, each representing a potential solution to the optimization problem. The critical parameters in PSO encompass the quantity of particles, the positional coordinates, and velocities of each particle within the solution space. Additionally, the neighborhood structure defines the communication patterns among particles, influencing their interactions and collective behavior during the optimization process. This topology communication plays a pivotal role in determining how particles exchange information, adapt their movements, and converge toward optimal solutions by leveraging both personal and neighborhood best positions. The PSO algorithm begins by initializing the population, with each particle assigned a random position and velocity within the solution space. Each particle’s position represents a possible solution, and its velocity indicates the direction and magnitude of its movement. The algorithm then evaluates the fitness values of all particles based on a predefined fitness function that quantifies how well a given solution solves the problem.

After evaluating fitness values, PSO updates the individual best position (p\_best) for each particle, which is the best solution the particle has encountered. Additionally, it updates the global best position (g\_best), which is the best solution found by any particle in the swarm. These updates guide the particles toward more promising regions of the solution space.

The velocity and position of each particle are then updated using the following equations:

  
$$\\begin{matrix} {v\_{id}^{t + 1} = v\_{id}^{t} + c\_{1}\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\,\\text{r}\\,\\,\\text{and}\\,\\,\\,\\left( \\left( ,{0,1}, \\right) \\right)\\,\\,\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\left( \\left( ,{p\_{id}^{t} - x\_{id}^{t}}, \\right) \\right) + c\_{2}\\,\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\,\\text{r}\\,\\,\\,\\text{and}\\,\\,\\,\\left( \\left( ,{0,1}, \\right) \\right)\\,\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\left( \\left( ,{p\_{gd}^{t} - x\_{id}^{t}}, \\right) \\right)} \\\\ {\\text{and}\\,\\,\\,\\, x\_{id}^{t + 1} = x\_{id}^{t} + \\,\\, v\_{id}^{t}} \\\\ \\end{matrix}$$  
(4.2) 

In Eq. (4.2), the terms are defined as follows:

*   *v*[*i**d*]^(*t*): Velocity of particle *i* in dimension *d* at iteration *t*.
*   *x*[*i**d*]^(*t*): Position of particle *i* in dimension *d* at iteration *t*.
*   *p*[*i*]: Best position found so far by particle *i*.
*   *p*[*g*]: Best position found so far among the particle’s neighbors.
*   *c*[1]: Scaling factor (often referred to as cognitive coefficient) governing the influence of *p*[*i*] on the particle’s movement.
*   *c*[2]: Scaling factor (often referred to as social coefficient) governing the influence of *p*[*g*] on the particle’s movement.
*   rand(0,1): Random number uniformly distributed between 0 and 1.

The exploration-exploitation balance in PSO is determined by comparing the difference between *p*[*i*] and *x*[*i**d*]^(*t*) and between *p*[*g*] and *x*[*i**d*]^(*t*):

*   Exploration takes place when a considerable disparity exists between the best individual position (*p*[*i*]) and the current position of particle *i* (*x*[*i**d*]^(*t*)), or between *p*[*g*] (best neighbor position) and *x*[*i**d*]^(*t*). This phase involves particles exploring new areas of the solution space to discover potentially better solutions beyond their current positions. It enables the algorithm to search for global optima by encouraging particles to venture into less explored regions, thereby enhancing the diversity of the search and preventing premature convergence to local optima.
*   Exploitation occurs when there is a small difference between these values, indicating that the particle is exploiting the local or global best positions.

These principles guide the movement of particles in the search space toward better solutions over successive iterations in the PSO algorithm. The PSO algorithm effectively combines local and global search strategies by leveraging the social behavior of particles. Its simplicity, ease of implementation, and ability to find high-quality solutions make it a popular choice for solving a wide range of optimization problems.

### 4.3.2 Grey Wolf Optimizer

The original GWO algorithm, introduced by Mirjalili et al. \[[17](#ch12.xhtml#r260)\], represents a significant advancement in the field of swarm intelligence-based algorithms. The GWO derives its principles from the intricate social structures and hunting dynamics exhibited by grey wolves in their natural environment. In this framework, the population of potential solutions is organized according to a strict hierarchy. The most optimal solution within the population is designated as the alpha (*α*) wolf, which leads the hunt and makes the most crucial decisions. The second and third most optimal solutions are termed beta (*β*) and delta (*δ*) wolves, respectively. These wolves assist the alpha in decision-making and strategy formation. All other solutions in the population are categorized as omega (*ω*) wolves, which follow the leaders and complete the hierarchy. This hierarchical structure allows the GWO to balance exploration and exploitation effectively during the optimization process. The algorithm mimics the grey wolves’ natural hunting process, including tracking, chasing, and attacking prey, to converge toward the optimal solution. This unique approach enables the GWO to perform robustly across various optimization problems.

In the hunting process, grey wolves simulate the encircling of their prey, which is a key aspect of their strategy for capturing food. This behavior is mathematically modeled in the GWO algorithm by the following equation:

  
$$\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}\\left( \\left( ,{t + 1}, \\right) \\right) = {\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{p}\\left( \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right) - \\overset{\\rightarrow}{A}\\,\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\,\\left| \\left| ,{\\,\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C}\\,\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\,{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{p}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right| - \\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right|}, \\right| \\right|$$  
(4.3) 

In this context, *t* represents the existing iteration index, coefficient vectors indicate with $\\overset{\\rightarrow}{A}$ and $\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C}$, and the position vector of the prey with ${\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{p}$. The expression $\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}$ denotes the position vector of a grey wolf. The encircling mechanism enables grey wolves to gradually approach and converge on their target (optimal solution) during the optimization process.

The coefficient vectors $\\overset{\\rightarrow}{A}$ and $\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C}$ are calculated using the following equations:

$\\overset{\\rightarrow}{A} = 2\\,\\overset{\\rightarrow}{a}\\,\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\,{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{r}}\_{1} - \\overset{\\rightarrow}{a}\\,\\,\\,\\,\\text{and}\\,\\,\\,\\,\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C} = 2{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{r}}\_{2}$(4.4) 

Here, *r*[1] and *r*[2] are random vectors with components drawn from the interval \[\[,0, 1,\]\]. The vector $\\overset{\\rightarrow}{a}$ a linearly decreases from 0 over the course of the iterations, following the equation:

$\\overset{\\rightarrow}{a}\\left( t \\right) = 2 - \\frac{2t}{\\text{MaxIter}}$(4.5) 

where MaxIter denotes the total number of iterations. This decrement helps balance exploration and exploitation by reducing the influence of the randomness as the algorithm progresses, thereby allowing the wolves to converge toward the optimal solution.

The grey wolves update their positions based on the positions of the alpha (*α*), beta (*β*), and delta (*δ*) wolves, which are the best solutions found so far. This hierarchical update strategy is critical to the GWO’s effectiveness, ensuring that the search process is guided by the best solutions at each iteration. The updated positions of the wolves are influenced by the positions of these top three wolves, which act as guides in the search space. This mechanism is designed to simulate the natural social hierarchy of grey wolves besides the collaborative hunting strategy, enabling the algorithm to efficiently explore and exploit the search space to find optimal solutions. The grey wolves update their positions based on the positions of **α**, *β*, and *δ* as follows:

  
$$\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}\\left( \\left( ,{t + 1}, \\right) \\right) = \\frac{1}{3}\\left( \\left( ,\\begin{array}{l} {{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{\\alpha}\\left( \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right) - {\\overset{\\rightarrow}{A}}\_{1}\\,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\left| \\left| ,{{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C}}\_{1}\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{\\alpha}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right| - \\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right|}, \\right| \\right|} \\\\ {+ {\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{\\beta}\\left( \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right) - {\\overset{\\rightarrow}{A}}\_{2\\,}\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\,\\left| \\left| ,{{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C}}\_{2\\,} \\bullet \\,{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{\\beta}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right| - \\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right|}, \\right| \\right|} \\\\ {+ {\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{\\delta}\\left( \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right) - {\\overset{\\rightarrow}{A}}\_{3}\\,\\,\\, \\bullet \\,\\,\\left| \\left| ,{{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C}}\_{3}\\,\\, \\bullet \\,{\\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}}\_{\\delta}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right| - \\overset{\\rightarrow}{X}\\left| \\left( ,t, \\right) \\right|}, \\right| \\right|} \\\\ \\end{array}, \\right) \\right)$$  
(4.6) 

where ${\\overset{\\rightarrow}{A}}\_{.}$ and ${\\overset{\\rightarrow}{C}}\_{.}$ are obtained as in Eq. (4.4), see \[[17](#ch12.xhtml#r260), [57](#ch12.xhtml#r300)\].

### 4.3.3 Genetic Algorithm

A genetic algorithm, inspired by Darwin’s theory of natural selection \[[58](#ch12.xhtml#r301),[59](#ch12.xhtml#r302)\], starts by selecting the fittest individuals from a population. These individuals reproduce, passing on their traits to the next generation. Offspring of fitter parents perform better, repeating the process to eventually yield the fittest generation. This approach applies to solving search problems by evaluating potential solutions and selecting the best ones. The genetic algorithm involves:

*   **Initial Population:** Individuals represent potential solutions encoded as binary strings of genes.
*   **Fitness Function**: Evaluates an individual’s competitiveness based on assigned fitness scores.
*   **Selection:** Fittest individuals are chosen for reproduction.
*   **Crossover:** Genes are exchanged between selected parents to create offspring.
*   **Mutation:** Occasional random changes maintain genetic diversity and prevent premature convergence.

### 4.3.4 Differential Evolution

DE was first conceptualized by Storn \[[60](#ch12.xhtml#r303)\], who also published the first publication on the topic as a technical report. By then, DE had become a popular choice among academics for addressing optimization challenges across various fields \[[61](#ch12.xhtml#r304)\]. Startup and evolution are the two phases of DE’s functioning. First, a population is formed at random. Then, during the evolution phase, the generated population is repeatedly subjected to the processes of mutation, crossover, and selection until a termination condition is met \[[62](#ch12.xhtml#r305)\]. The algorithmic procedure of DE can be summarized as follows. Individuals are selected randomly from intervals \[*x*[*j*]^(*L*),*x*[*j*]^(*U*)\], where *x*[*j*]^(*L*) and *x*[*j*]^(*U*) represent the lower and upper bounds of decision parameters. Each of these selected individuals then undergoes mutation, recombination, and subsequent selection. Mutation plays a crucial role in expanding the search space. For any given individual *u*, three distinct parameters *i*, *r*[1], and *r*[2] and *r*[3] are randomly chosen. The donor vector *r*[*i* + 1] is computed as *r*[*i* + 1] = *r*[1] + *α*(*r*[2]−*r*[3]), where *α*, termed the mutation factor, ranges between 0 and 2. This process enhances diversity and exploration within the population. These three parameters are replaced to selected individual *u* if the r  and ()>  CR, where CR is a constant probability in *U*\[0,1\]. If the fitness function of the updated individual is less than the original individual *u*, then *u* is removed from the set of individuals, and an updated one is replaced. Until a stopping condition is met, mutation, recombination, and selection proceed.

## 4.4 Statement of WSN Coverage Optimization

The sensor node’s coverage area is defined as a circular region with a constant radius, determining the inclusion of locations within its operational scope. This metric is essential for evaluating how effectively a WSN monitors a two-dimensional (2D) area. To quantify the network’s coverage rate across this monitoring zone, the entire space is systematically divided into *M* × *N* pixels. The coverage rate itself is defined as the ratio of covered points (*x*) to the total number of pixels (*M* × *N*), providing a measure of how comprehensively the WSN can detect and monitor activity within the designated area. This evaluation assumes uniform sensor characteristics throughout and simplifies the analysis by disregarding potential influences from the boundaries of the monitored region.

In a monitoring area with *N* sensor nodes, each with a perceived radius *R*, denoted as *T*  = {{,*t*[1],  *t*[2],  …,  *t*[*N*],}}, the coordinates of the *i*\-th sensor node *t*[*i*] are ((,*x*[*i*],  *y*[*i*],)). Similarly, the coordinates of the *j*\-th monitoring node *h* are *x*[*j*],  *y*[*j*]. The distance between *h* and *t*[*i*] is expressed by the following equation:

$d\\left( \\left( ,{t\_{i} - h}, \\right) \\right) = \\left( \\left( ,{\\left( \\left( ,{x\_{i} - \\,\\, x\_{j}}, \\right) \\right)^{2} + \\,\\,\\left( \\left( ,{y\_{i} - \\,\\, y\_{j}}, \\right) \\right)^{2}}, \\right) \\right)^{\\frac{1}{2}}$(4.7) 

This study adopts a binary perception paradigm, where the likelihood of sensor *t*[*i*] detecting monitoring node *h* is quantified by the following probability expression:

$p\\left( \\left( ,{t\_{i} - h}, \\right) \\right) = \\left\\{ \\left\\{ ,\\begin{matrix} {1,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\, d\\left\\{ \\left( ,{t\_{i} - h}, \\right) \\right. \\leq 0} \\\\ {0,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\,\\, d\\left\\{ \\left( ,{t\_{i} - h}, \\right) \\right. > 0} \\\\ \\end{matrix} \\right. \\right.$(4.8) 

Sensor *t*[*i*] effectively covers monitoring point *h* when *p*(*t*[*i*]−*h*) = 1. Conversely, if *p*(*t*[*i*]−*h*) = 0, sensor *t*[*i*] does not cover *h*. Given that multiple sensor nodes may concurrently observe the same position within the monitoring region, the probability that monitoring point *h* is detected by the node set *T* can be expressed as:

$p\\left( {T,h} \\right) = 1 - \\prod\\limits\_{t\_{i} \\in T}\\left( {1 - \\left( {p\\left( {t\_{i} - h} \\right)} \\right)} \\right)$(4.9) 

The percent coverage rate of the node set *T* over the entire monitoring area is:

$R\_{c} = \\frac{\\sum\_{h \\in M \\times N}p\\left( {T,h} \\right)}{M \\times N} \\times 100$(4.10) 

In this chapter, to test optimizers in a more challenging design space, we assume some obstacle areas over the whole design area *M* × *N*. [Figure 4.1](#ch12.xhtml#f3) shows the schematic 2D map (100 × 100) of the test area used in the benchmark functions employed in the current comparative study.

![Figure 4.1 Schematic representation of network area with obstacles. The free pixels (not including the obstacles’ coordinates) are considered a network area that needs to be covered by sensor nodes and design space in optimization models.](html/images/9781032751245_C004_001.jpg)

Figure 4.1 Schematic representation of network area with obstacles. The free pixels (not including the obstacles’ coordinates) are considered a network area that needs to be covered by sensor nodes and design space in optimization models.

Let’s assume the obstacle areas are not interested to be covered by sensor nodes, and just *M* × *N* − *o**b**s**t**a**c**l**e**s* are targeted to be covered. Therefore, Eq. (4.10) can be replaced by:

$R\_{c} = \\frac{\\sum\_{h \\in Mf \\times Nf}p\\left( {T,h} \\right)}{Mf \\times Nf} \\times 100$(4.11) 

where *M**f* × *N**f* are coordinates (pixels) of free area in *XY* direction (2D) map that does not include pixels of obstacles’ areas. Here, the fitness function for the WSN coverage optimization problem is considered based on the formulation in Eq. (4.11).

## 4.5 Systematic Comparison of Optimizers

### 4.5.1 Simulation Setup

In this study, six distinct cases of WSN coverage optimization problems are investigated to compare different optimizers, as detailed in [Table 4.1](#ch12.xhtml#t1).

Table 4.1 Six Different Problems Are Employed in the Comparison of Optimizers

 
| **Case** **No.** || **Number of Sensors** || **Max, Function Evaluations of Optimizers** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 || 15 || 150 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2 || 15 || 300 |
| 3 || 15 || 600 |
| 4 || 30 || 300 |
| 5 || 30 || 600 |
| 6 || 30 || 1,200 |

These cases vary in terms of the number of sensor nodes and the maximum number of function evaluations tailored for each optimizer. In the 2D monitoring area, the search agent’s dimension (number of design variables) doubles the number of sensor nodes along both the *X*\-direction and *Y*\-direction. For instance, cases with 15 sensor nodes involve 30 design variables in the first, second, and third cases, and 60 variables in the fourth, fifth, and sixth cases.

To ensure optimal convergence within the specified number of function evaluations, the maximum number of iterations for each optimizer is adjusted accordingly. For example, the maximum iterations initially set for the optimizers based on the number of function evaluations are 5, 10, 20, 5, 10, and 20 for cases 1–6 in [Table 4.1](#ch12.xhtml#t1), respectively.

During the initialization phase, the initial coordinates of all sensor nodes are set as the initial input values for the optimizers, corresponding to the initial population size. These sensor nodes are randomly distributed across the designated area, with each search agent in every algorithm representing a sensor node placement technique.

The results yielded by every optimization model comprise two crucial aspects: the optimal percentage coverage rate, which measures the extent of the monitored area effectively covered by the WSN, and the precise spatial coordinates of each sensor node deployed within the network. These outcomes are essential for evaluating the performance and effectiveness of each optimization strategy in enhancing WSN coverage capabilities. The simulations are conducted using Matlab^®/R2022b to construct and simulate each problem’s optimization model. To ensure robust comparison results, each problem undergoes evaluation with 10 individual repetitions across the six different cases.

In these experiments, the monitoring area measures 100 × 100  m, with a sensor’s perceived coverage radius set at 10 m, and the network area divided into 10,000 pixels. However, to add complexity and challenge the optimizers further, some pixels are designated as obstacles within the network area, as illustrated in [Figure 4.1](#ch12.xhtml#f3). Specifically, 1,636 pixels are obstructed, leaving 8,362 pixels monitored by the coordinates of monitoring and sensor nodes.

The fitness function for optimizers is based on the percent coverage rate of the WSN, formulated as Eq. (4.11), with the objective of maximizing this fitness function. The study focuses on evaluating the performance of four optimizers—PSO, GWO, GA, and DE—in solving each of the six optimization problems.

### 4.5.2 Results and Discussion

For each specific case outlined in [Table 4.1](#ch12.xhtml#t1) and detailed in the previous section, all four optimizers—PSO, GWO, GA, and DE—are systematically evaluated. Each optimizer is tasked with optimizing the placement of sensor nodes within the network area, aiming to maximize the percent coverage rate as the fitness function. The results obtained include the optimal positions of all sensor nodes and the corresponding optimal fitness function values.

To ensure the reliability of the findings, each optimization problem is repeated 10 times with identical conditions, allowing for a comprehensive evaluation of each optimizer’s performance. The evaluation criteria encompass three key metrics: average fitness function values across all repetitions (accuracy), standard deviation (Std) of fitness function values across repetitions (robustness), and total computational time required for each optimizer across all repetitions.

This rigorous approach enables a thorough analysis of how each metaheuristic performs under various conditions and problem complexities. The results obtained from each case are meticulously examined and discussed in the subsequent sections to provide insights into the effectiveness and efficiency of each optimizer in addressing WSN coverage optimization challenges.

*   **Case 1:**

In the first case, which involves optimizing the placement of 15 sensor nodes, each optimizer operates with 30 design variables corresponding to the coordinates of these sensors. This scenario is characterized by the lowest number of function evaluations allotted to each optimizer, specifically 150 evaluations. Consequently, optimizations in this case are configured with a fixed number of five iterations, and an initial population size set to 30.

Additionally, specific parameters are carefully selected for PSO. The velocity clamping factor, cognitive constant, and social constant are uniformly set to 2. The inertia weight varies dynamically between 0.4 and 0.9, ensuring a balance between exploration and exploitation throughout the optimization process.

The optimization process using each optimizer of PSO, GWO, GA, and DE has been separately conducted over ten individual repetitions. According to the obtained result, the relevant box plot, total elapsed time, and representation of obtained fitness function (coverage rate), by different optimizers over ten individual repetitions are provided in [Figure 4.2](#ch12.xhtml#f4).

![Figure 4.2 Box plot, total elapsed time, and representation of obtained fitness function (coverage rate) by different optimizers over 10 individual repetitions for the first case in the optimization problem of WSN coverage.](html/images/9781032751245_C004_002.jpg)

Figure 4.2 Box plot, total elapsed time, and representation of obtained fitness function (coverage rate) by different optimizers over 10 individual repetitions for the first case in the optimization problem of WSN coverage.

The statistical results by different optimizers in the first case of the WSN coverage optimization problem are summarized in [Table 4.2](#ch12.xhtml#t2).

Table 4.2 Statistical Outcomes Achieved by Various Optimizers in the Case 1 WSN Coverage Optimization Problem

 
| **Method** || **Ave** || **Std** || **Max** || **Min** || **Time Elapsed (s)** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PSO || 41.24 || 1.22 || 43.16 || 39.54 || 13.97 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| GWO || 41.44 || 1.50 || 44.26 || 39.60 || 13.83 |
| GA || 44.34 || 1.50 || 46.15 || 41.56 || 16.97 |
| DE || 42.45 || 1.53 || 45.62 || 40.89 || 16.60 |

It is apparent from this table that the GA algorithm provides a higher coverage rate followed by DE in the second rank. Both GWO and PSO provided competitive results as their fourth rank to obtain the best fitness function. Despite higher accuracy by GA, however, this algorithm elapsed much more computational time compared with other alternatives to investigate the optimal coordinates of sensor nodes. The GWO method is conducted a bit faster than the three other methods.

*   **Case 2:**

In this particular case, we maintain consistency by considering the same configuration of 15 sensor nodes within the model. As in the previous scenario, each optimizer addresses 30 design variables and employs an identical initial population setup. However, in this instance, we have increased the maximum number of iterations to 10 for all methods, resulting in a total of 300 function evaluations. The other parameters for all optimizers remain unchanged from the previous case to ensure a fair comparison.

The optimization process is rigorously executed 10 times using each optimizer independently to ensure the robustness and reliability of the results. Detailed analyses of the outcomes, including comprehensive box plots, detailed representations of total elapsed time, and graphical depictions of the fitness function (coverage rate) achieved by various optimizers across these 10 individual runs, are provided in [Figure 4.3](#ch12.xhtml#f5) and summarized in [Table 4.3](#ch12.xhtml#t3).

| **Method** || **Ave** || **Std** || **Max.** || **Min.** || **Time Elapsed (s)** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PSO || 40.90 || 1.74 || 44.12 || 38.57 || 41.51 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| GWO || 42.34 || 1.56 || 44.38 || 40.27 || 39.53 |
| GA || 45.52 || 0.71 || 46.80 || 44.48 || 45.41 |
| DE || 43.16 || 1.39 || 45.41 || 40.86 || 43.63 |

Upon careful examination of these results, it becomes evident that the performance of all optimizers remains consistently competitive compared to case 1. Despite the significant increase in the number of function evaluations from 150 in the previous case to 300 in the current case, the observed improvement in coverage rate across all algorithms is marginal, hovering around 1%. These findings underscore the nuanced balance between computational effort and achievable optimization gains in WSN coverage optimization problems.

*   **Case 3:**

The same number of sensor nodes (15 sensors) is considered in this case; however, the allowable number of function evaluations for all optimizers amounted to 600. The same as in both previous cases, the optimization procedure is adjusted, while the maximum iteration is set to 20. The optimization results for 10 individual repetitions using each of the four optimizers are obtained. | **Method** || **Ave** || **Std** || **Max** || **Min** || **Time Elapsed (s)** |

It is apparent from this table that the GA optimizer still provided a higher coverage rate (best fitness function), however with the highest elapsed time. The second better performance belonged to the GWO optimizer, followed by DE and PSO. The PSO can obtain the optimal solution with the smallest elapsed computational time.

In contrast to the preceding three cases, the fourth through sixth scenarios involve an increase in the number of sensor nodes to 30. The initial population size for each optimizer is accordingly adjusted to 60 individuals. In this case, the maximum number of iterations is set to 5 to accommodate a total of 300 function evaluations. The optimization process is conducted across 10 individual runs, and the resultant findings are graphically depicted in [Figure 4.5](#ch12.xhtml#f7).

The first three cases with 15 sensor nodes and the second three cases (cases 4, 5, and 6) are depicted separately. The important point that can be seen from these plots is that the increasing number of function evaluations and accordingly elapsing time for optimization procedure do not result in significant improvement in the fitness function (coverage rate) for the current cases of WSN coverage optimization problems. All four metaheuristics represent very competitive results, while GA provides much better results overall.

## 4.6 Conclusion

The extensive utilization of metaheuristic algorithms for optimizing WSN coverage remains an area of active exploration within the research community. This chapter contributes to this ongoing discourse through a comprehensive comparative study that systematically evaluates the efficacy of four widely employed metaheuristic techniques: PSO, GWO, GA, and DE. These algorithms were rigorously tested across various configurations of the WSN coverage optimization problem, yielding insights into their respective strengths and limitations.

Throughout the comparative analysis, all four metaheuristics demonstrated competitive and comparable performance metrics. While no single method emerged as definitively superior for all scenarios, GA exhibited a marginally stronger capability in achieving higher coverage rates in certain contexts. However, it was noted that GA tended to operate at a slower pace compared to PSO, GWO, and DE, particularly evident in scenarios involving extensive computational iterations.

A notable finding from this study was the nuanced relationship between the number of function evaluations, total optimization time, and the resulting coverage rates. Contrary to expectations, increasing computational resources did not uniformly lead to significant enhancements in coverage performance across all tested scenarios. This observation underscores the importance of algorithmic efficiency and strategic deployment in achieving optimal WSN coverage.

In conclusion, while GA demonstrated potential advantages in specific optimization contexts, the choice of metaheuristic should be tailored to the specific requirements and constraints of the WSN application under consideration. Future research directions may explore hybridization strategies or novel algorithmic adaptations to further refine and enhance the efficiency and effectiveness of metaheuristic approaches in WSN coverage optimization.
