# chapter 5Fundamentals of Machine Learning in Communication Systems

Shivani Sharma and Avani Vyas

DOI: [10.1201/9781003472506-5](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003472506-5)

## 5.1 Introduction

The demands for security, availability, and performance of new applications, services, and devices are escalating at a pace beyond initial expectations. Real-time responsiveness is crucial in application domains such as e-health, traffic management, and industry operations, necessitating communication networks to autonomously make real-time decisions. Achieving this level of autonomy entails networks that can react to and learn from their environment, effectively controlling themselves without human intervention. However, conventional communication networks have historically followed a different trajectory, relying on manual human responses to factors like traffic fluctuations, updates in network functions, security incidents, and faults. This reliance on human-machine interactions has resulted in network downtime, increased security vulnerabilities, and numerous challenges within current communication networks.

The necessity for human interaction or manual configuration presents a significant obstacle for networks to leverage their past experiences to adapt to changing requirements. The overarching concept involves predicting the future behavior of services, network segments, users, or user equipment (UE) and dynamically tuning the network based on this predictive information. For example, predicting the movement trajectory of a user can be accomplished through straightforward mechanisms like measuring signal strength between consecutive base stations to minimize handover latency. Furthermore, the evolving landscape of services and networks, such as vehicular communication and the internet of things (IoT) in the fifth generation (5G), underscores the need for the introduction of intelligent network operations. Intelligence in communication networks is characterized by an autonomous pursuit of goals without human intervention, especially in the face of uncertainty arising from a lack of information. The system must actively react to and adapt to changes in its environment, learning from experience through information gathered during its operation.

Machine learning (ML) systems can be broadly classified into several categories, including supervised learning, unsupervised learning, semi-supervised learning, and reinforcement learning. In supervised learning, the system predicts output data based on labeled training data, while unsupervised learning focuses on identifying patterns and associations within input data. Semi-supervised learning involves training with a combination of labeled and unlabeled input data, aiming to either label the unannotated data or predict outputs for new input data. In reinforcement learning, the system learns to take actions that maximize cumulative rewards.

These ML techniques empower systems with intelligence, enhancing their performance based on available information. In the context of communication networks, integrating ML disciplines, technologies, and methods becomes imperative. This integration helps mitigate risks associated with human control, enabling networks to autonomously adapt, self-control, and respond to dynamic conditions.

ML can also contribute to improving the performance of cognitive radios in spectrum sharing and optimizing resources in heterogeneous access networks. Emerging technologies like software-defined networking (SDN), network function virtualization (NFV), and multi-access edge computing (MEC) offer dynamic network operations but come with challenges, such as potential bottlenecks in centralized control platforms. Investigating mechanisms that enable networks to learn from their environment, understand challenges, and dynamically configure themselves is crucial, and ML stands out as a promising candidate for providing such capabilities in communication networks.

## 5.2 ML for Physical Layer

The physical layer, often denoted as Layer 1, represents the foundational level within a communication system. It is responsible for overseeing the efficient transmission, receiver processing, and precise modeling of channels to guarantee the dependable transmission of data across physical channels. The development of communication signal processing algorithms for the physical layer typically involves analytical design techniques, utilizing mathematical optimization, statistics, and information theory. In this section, certain key domains for ML applications in the physical layer are discussed in detail.

### 5.2.1 Application of Neural Networks for Digital Pre-distortion

Non-linear distortion poses significant challenges for digital systems, primarily stemming from high-power amplifiers (HPAs) in digital radio systems. While linear class A amplifiers and operating the HPA away from saturation are conventional solutions, they result in expensive and inefficient systems. A more efficient approach involves employing a non-linear filter at the transmitter, creating the inverse of an HPA response, known as digital pre-distortion (DPD). In the depicted typical transmitter setup, the baseband input stream a(KT0) undergoes pre-distortion, yielding the signal b(KT0), which then passes through modulator and demodulator pulse shaping filters with impulse responses gT(t) and gR(t). The impact of the HPA is assessed by comparing the demodulator output c(KT0) with the pre-distortion filter output b(KT0). The pre-distortion filter’s weight adjusts based on the disparity between c(KT0) and b(KT0). However, the accuracy of the pre-distortion filter hinges on the precision of the HPA model. Traditional memory polynomial models may falter due to the HPA’s non-linear continuous function response, especially when accommodating various signal types. Artificial neural networks (ANNs) emerge as an efficient tool for implementing non-linear mappings. With sufficient neurons, feed-forward ANNs act as universal approximators for arbitrary continuous functions, making them a natural choice for pre-distortion, trainable to generalize HPA models across different carrier types.

In reference \[[1](#ch13.xhtml#r306)\], an ANN-based pre-distortion technique for satellite communications was introduced by the author. The proposed DPD method comprises two distinct ANNs. The initial ANN is employed for modeling the transfer function of the traveling wave tube (TWT) amplifier, while the second ANN is utilized for the inverse of the transfer function. Both ANNs are structured with two layers, each consisting of *N* = 10 neurons. In contrast to using a known normalized TWT, the authors assumed a non-normalized solid-state HPA with unspecified parameters and intrinsic adaptive behavior in a different study \[[2](#ch13.xhtml#r307)\]. Each ANN in this case is composed of a hidden layer with *N* = 10 neurons and an output layer with a single neuron.

### 5.2.2 Learning to Decode

Channel coding is a strategy employed to manage errors in data communication over noisy channels. The utilization of ANNs in the realm of channel coding and decoding was first introduced by Bruck and Blaum in 1989 \[[3](#ch13.xhtml#r308)\]. Their pioneering work illustrated the construction of an ANN, associated with an error-correction code, wherein each local maximum is representative of a codeword, and vice versa. Subsequent research, detailed in Zeng et al. \[[4](#ch13.xhtml#r309)\], demonstrated the practical application of ANN in the decoding of error-correction codes. Furthermore, Caid and Means \[[5](#ch13.xhtml#r310)\] delved into the implementation of ANN for channel decoders. The proposed ANN-based decoders exhibited superior performance compared to traditional counterparts when faced with scenarios where assumptions, such as additive white Gaussian noise (AWGN) or binary symmetric channel (BSC), were violated, such as in jamming environments. A departure from conventional methods was proposed by using an ANN for Hamming codes, allocating each output node to a codeword instead of individual bits. The authors explored potential solutions for Hamming codes using two distinct ANNs: the counter-propagation neural network and back-propagation.

An alternative approach is presented in Liang et al. \[[6](#ch13.xhtml#r311)\], which proposes an iterative belief propagation scheme employing a convolutional neural network (CNN) architecture. Here, a conventional belief propagation decoder estimates coded bits, and a CNN is subsequently employed to rectify estimation errors. In Lyu et al. \[[7](#ch13.xhtml#r312)\], a comparative analysis is conducted for channel decoding, evaluating the performance of multilayer perceptron (MLP), CNN, and recurrent neural network (RNN). The findings reveal that RNN exhibits superior decoding performance, albeit with increased complexity. Additionally, the study notes that the codeword length significantly influences the adaptability of ANNs, coining the term “saturation length” to denote the point at which learning becomes restricted.

### 5.2.3 Neurobeamformer

Beamforming is a signal processing technique crucial for directional signal transmission and reception. It revolves around adjusting the phase angles of an antenna array to create constructive interference for signals at a specific angle, thereby focusing the signal in the desired direction. The calculation of phase angles typically takes place in the digital domain, forming an integral part of the physical layer. Unlike pre-equalization or pre-coding in MIMO systems, beamformers involve steering a beam toward a specific direction. The historical evolution of ANN-based beamformers traces back to the 1980s. [Figure 5.1](#ch13.xhtml#f13) depicts a typical configuration of a neurobeamformer.

![Figure 5.1 A typical setup of a neurobeamformer where an ANN is used to predict the beamforming weights.](html/images/9781032751245_C005_001.jpg)

Figure 5.1 A typical setup of a neurobeamformer where an ANN is used to predict the beamforming weights.

ANN is employed to predict the beamforming weights. This neurobeamformer utilizes the ANN to fine-tune the angles of the phased arrays, ensuring that transmitted signals from various antennas converge precisely in the target user’s direction. The concept of radial basis function artificial neural network (RBF ANN) is expanded upon by Southall et al. \[[8](#ch13.xhtml#r313)\]. In this work, the authors employ an adaptive RBF ANN trained through a gradient descent algorithm and a linear algebra-based network using an LMS error solution. While the ANN beamformers demonstrate high performance, challenges arise with data subjected to severe near-field scattering conditions.

Lovato and Gong \[[9](#ch13.xhtml#r314)\], introduced a novel ANN structure for implementing antenna array beamforming. This ANN comprises two hidden layers, with the first layer divided into sublayers equal to the number of inputs. These sublayers are fully connected to the second hidden layer. The ANN is trained using data from minimum variance distortionless response (MVDR) beamforming. The proposed ANN structure surpasses conventional ANN structures in terms of beamforming performance.

## 5.3 ML for Medium Access Control Layer

In wireless networks, the spectrum is a limited resource, necessitating a robust medium access control (MAC) protocol that facilitates channel access for multiple users. The objective is to maximize spectrum utilization and ensure quality of service (QoS). As the number of connected devices continues to rise, expanding the optimization domain with an increasing number of parameters and stringent QoS requirements, MAC protocols are expected to face unprecedented complexity. The MAC protocol’s intricacy depends on factors such as network architecture, communication model, and duplex mechanisms (e.g., time division duplexing (TDD), frequency division duplexing (FDD), and full duplexing). The constant evolution of wireless networks, introducing new technological components like flexible duplexing, adaptive frame numerologies, and the convergence of heterogeneous wireless networks with multi-interface radio devices, further complicates MAC tasks.

Viewed as a large-scale control problem with diverse QoS constraints, optimizing MAC with traditional rule-based algorithms is suboptimal. Current algorithms for MAC radio resource allocation often adopt an optimization approach that makes assumptions to simplify the non-convex problem, resulting in suboptimal solutions due to the complexity that hinders solving the full non-convex problem.

Furthermore, wireless networks generate abundant data through continuous measurements from user devices and network entities in the form of radio and system data. However, existing MAC protocols underutilize this data, considering it short-lived and localized due to aging and user mobility. The evolution of machine learning (ML) and wireless networks presents an opportunity to leverage such data in multiple dimensions, creating data-driven wireless networks that are more robust and autonomous in dynamic environments. This section provides a comprehensive overview of potential research directions and associated challenges arising from the integration of ML algorithms into MAC protocol design for future wireless networks.

### 5.3.1 Interference Prediction

The transmission of signals by multiple devices in the same frequency within the shared medium of air disrupts signal reception, a phenomenon known as “interference.” Despite advancements in modeling interference dynamics in wireless networks using approaches like interference alignment \[[10](#ch13.xhtml#r315)\], researchers have struggled to develop algorithms that fully leverage interference knowledge to enhance the QoS of communication.

Interference alignment’s fundamental concept involves coordinating multiple transmitters to align mutual interference at the receiver. However, ongoing efforts explore the integration of machine learning, such as using an autoregressive (AR) model, where the next state is predicted based on past states, to enhance the performance of interference alignment algorithms \[[11](#ch13.xhtml#r316)\]. A key challenge in utilizing interference information lies in its randomness and short-time validity due to mobility. Additionally, identifying the exact source of interference among multiple sources, coupled with the random nature of interferers at each time instance, further compounds the complexity of the problem.

### 5.3.2 User Mobility and Association

Numerous researchers have delved into employing machine learning (ML) techniques for localization, aiming to enhance algorithm accuracy by training them with phase information and estimating the angle of arrival through ML using phase fingerprinting \[[12](#ch13.xhtml#r317)–[15](#ch13.xhtml#r320)\]. In Ezpeleta et al. \[[12](#ch13.xhtml#r317)\], an indoor positioning algorithm based on KNN utilizes historical data for estimating the mobile user’s position. Similarly, the algorithm presents a positioning estimation algorithm based on deep neural network (DNN). This algorithm employs a stacked denoising autoencoder (SDA) and a hidden-Markov model (HMM) to minimize the feature set, smoothing the original location without compromising information content.

Zhang et al. \[[13](#ch13.xhtml#r318)\] combine support vector machine (SVM) and ANN to estimate user position based on the received signal strength indicator (RSSI). However, the focus of this work is on boundary-level localization rather than the precise user position. In Berruet et al. \[[15](#ch13.xhtml#r320)\], a CNN-based fingerprinting technique for indoor localization based on channel frequency response (CFR) is introduced. Similarly, another scheme has been proposed, i.e. deep long short-term memory (LF-DLSTM) approach for indoor localization based on RSSI, reducing noise effects to improve positioning accuracy.

However, a prevalent issue in many proposed positioning algorithms, whether based on ML techniques or conventional methods like OTDOA, UTDOA, RSSI, and Cell-ID, is the reliance on single value estimates (SVEs) such as phase information, angle of arrival, or RSSI. Localization estimates heavily depend on the quality of these SVEs, which degrade or exhibit high randomness in the wireless environment due to factors like multipath and non-line-of-sight (NLOS). To fully leverage the potential of learning techniques in terms of user mobility and localization, an avenue worth exploring is cooperative positioning techniques through data fusion of 5G with other sources like cameras, device-to-device (D2D) links, GPS, etc. Additionally, optimizing communication and positioning targets jointly, as they often overlap, represents a promising direction.

### 5.3.3 Radio Resource Scheduling

Machine learning (ML) algorithms offer innovative solutions for addressing asymmetric traffic accommodation in wireless networks, particularly by enhancing the flexible duplexing capabilities of 5G networks—a promising technique for optimizing resources based on traffic demand in both downlink and uplink \[[16](#ch13.xhtml#r321)\]. Flexible duplexing aims to achieve adaptive resource allocation considering the asymmetric uplink and downlink traffic, optimizing in the time-frequency domain and blurring the distinction between TDD and FDD, or even eliminating it entirely. In Malik et al. \[[17](#ch13.xhtml#r322)\], the author introduces radio resource allocation based on flexible duplexing to mitigate inter-cell interference.

To fully harness the potential of flexible duplexing and adaptively optimize resources, predicting or learning the traffic behavior of the network becomes crucial. Numerous researchers have explored traffic flow prediction using linear models such as autoregressive (AR), autoregressive moving average (ARMA), and moving average (MA) \[[18](#ch13.xhtml#r323),[19](#ch13.xhtml#r324)\], achieving success in small-scale sparse networks. However, considering the evolving nature of wireless networks, many non-linear factors, such as multi-dependency and abruptness, significantly impact performance. Therefore, recent efforts have focused on machine learning-based models involving ANNs, SVM, least-square support vector machines (LSSVM), long short-term memory (LSTM), etc. \[[20](#ch13.xhtml#r325)\]. These models learn from historical traffic data to understand randomness and improve traffic flow prediction. In Zhao et al. \[[20](#ch13.xhtml#r325)\], a 2D-LSTM model enables self-optimization and an intelligent response for fault tolerance, showcasing the ability of ML techniques to provide self-X features essential for HetNets.

Moreover, future wireless systems like NB-IoT and LTE-M are expected to adopt symmetric frequency allocation between downlink and uplink due to half-duplex frequency division duplex (HD-FDD). However, such symmetric allocation may lead to underutilization of spectrum resources, given the asymmetric nature of traffic. ML techniques can intelligently address such challenges, especially in IoT networks, where uplink data traffic is anticipated to be higher than downlink, potentially avoiding resource wastage. Similarly, in broadband communication, where downlink traffic prevails, ML techniques can offer intelligent solutions to seamlessly integrate environmental parameters and enable proactive MAC functionalities, departing from traditional reactive approaches.

## 5.4 ML for Network Layer

In the realm of network research, the network layer assumes a pivotal role in orchestrating the end-to-end delivery of packets through the calculation of routes across the network \[[21](#ch13.xhtml#r326)\]. As the landscape of communication entities grows increasingly complex and diverse, the imperative arises for the network to autonomously configure routing nodes with minimal human intervention. This configuration involves adjusting the behavior of network devices, such as routers and switches, to facilitate the seamless delivery of traffic from various applications. However, this heightened complexity introduces a set of challenges.

Traditional network traffic control systems often rely on rigid, pre-defined policies, rendering the network less adaptable to dynamic environments and susceptible to stagnation. This predicament becomes particularly pronounced in wireless networks, where abrupt shifts in traffic patterns and volumes are commonplace. Consequently, there is a pressing need for novel mechanisms to ensure resilient and optimal end-to-end packet delivery. Within the realm of network research, machine learning (ML) has emerged as one of the most effective mechanisms for addressing and surmounting these challenges in network configuration and traffic control.

### 5.4.1 Network Traffic Control

Effective control of network traffic is essential for optimizing overall network performance, whether in fixed networks \[[22](#ch13.xhtml#r327)\], wireless networks \[[23](#ch13.xhtml#r328),[24](#ch13.xhtml#r329)\], or virtual networks \[[25](#ch13.xhtml#r330)\]. Network traffic engineering plays a crucial role in reducing congestion and enhancing utilization by distributing the load across multiple paths. By doing so, it enables communication networks to deliver data optimally between two points, even in the event of link or router failures. However, the prevalent approach of independently performing failure recovery and traffic engineering in today’s networks often leads to complex and compromised traffic control systems \[[26](#ch13.xhtml#r331)\]. The evolving landscape of novel services and devices necessitates automated network traffic control systems, prompting the application of ML-based approaches for efficiency.

Deep learning (DL) has been explored for intelligent traffic control, as highlighted by Fadlullah et al. \[[27](#ch13.xhtml#r332)\], where the authors provide an overview of state-of-the-art DL architectures and algorithms relevant to network traffic control systems. The core function of a network traffic control system is to efficiently route packets between two entities. Traditional routing protocols, being non-intelligent, cannot learn from past events for autonomous decision-making \[[28](#ch13.xhtml#r333)\]. Consequently, there is a shift toward replacing conventional network protocols, reliant on algorithmic insights from human experts, with data-driven, predictive, or ML-based approaches. Traffic steering is another avenue where ML techniques can be employed to avoid congested paths in the network or maximize QoS among existing alternative paths, as exemplified by Adachi et al. \[[29](#ch13.xhtml#r334)\] through Q-learning. [Figure 5.2](#ch13.xhtml#f14) presents a simple network traffic control system using ML, where traffic patterns are used to train the system for optimal routing paths.

![Figure 5.2 General learning approaches for network traffic control.](html/images/9781032751245_C005_002.jpg)

Figure 5.2 General learning approaches for network traffic control.

The subsequent sub-sections delve into ML-aided routing protocols tailored for diverse network architectures and networking technologies.

### 5.4.2 Routing

The application of ML for routing has been explored by Valadarsky et al. \[[30](#ch13.xhtml#r335)\]. The primary focus of this work is to assess whether ML can be effectively employed to automatically generate optimal routing configurations. The study, framed within the context of intra-domain traffic engineering \[[31](#ch13.xhtml#r336)\], serves as a case study. It delves into ML-guided routing optimization within a single, self-administered network, with specific attention given to (i) framing routing as an ML problem, and (ii) identifying suitable representations for input and output within the learning domains. The authors conclude that data-driven routing, or ML-based routing, holds significant potential for enhancing network performance but can also pose notable challenges if not applied judiciously. These challenges relate to network overhead and delay, particularly when proper techniques and learning parameters are not meticulously selected.

The effectiveness of ML in improving the quality of transmission in optical networks is demonstrated by Salani et al. \[[32](#ch13.xhtml#r337)\]. The proposed integrated approach for routing and spectrum assignment leverages probabilistic outputs of ML-based quality of transmission. This approach results in saving up to 30% of the spectrum and a reduction in the number of risky lightpaths—those exceeding a certain threshold of bit error rate.

In the domain of traffic prediction, Musumeci et al. \[[33](#ch13.xhtml#r338)\] explore supervised learning mechanisms utilizing historical data and user profiles. These mechanisms prove effective in predicting future traffic requirements and the corresponding resource needs.

### 5.4.3 Routing in Dynamic (AD-HOC) Networks

In Sharma et al. \[[34](#ch13.xhtml#r339)\], an ML-based protocol for efficient routing in opportunistic networks is proposed. This routing protocol enhances the performance of the PROPHET+ protocol \[[35](#ch13.xhtml#r340)\] by integrating ML techniques to self-train based on various factors, including hop count, buffer capacity, node energy, node moving speed, node popularity, and the number of successful deliveries. The ML algorithm utilizes past routing data to assess a node’s capability to deliver messages to intended destinations. Two ML models, ANN and decision trees (DTs), are employed to calculate the probability of correct message delivery. The proposed protocol enhances delivery probability while minimizing average latency, overhead, and buffer size requirements.

Similarly, in Zhi-yuan et al. \[[36](#ch13.xhtml#r341)\], the wavelet neural network prediction model is utilized for multipath routing in wireless mesh networks, demonstrating improved adaptability and robustness to link failures and congestion. However, conventional ML techniques, such as ANNs, exhibit limitations in terms of scalability and computation efficiency when considered for routing, as discussed in Kato et al. \[[37](#ch13.xhtml#r342)\]. Therefore, incorporating additional resource constraints through ML in routing on the data plane or routing devices could exacerbate existing congestion and latency challenges. It is crucial to evaluate the cost versus benefits before adopting ML-based techniques for routing.

## 5.5 ML for Edge Computing

The prevailing architecture for mobile and IoT services consists of a three-tier structure, incorporating local user interfacing, sensor data collection, and actuation at the edge layer, while service and application logic are centralized in data centers positioned between the cloud and devices. This three-tier model offers advantages, such as resource efficiency at the local level and global service availability. However, the edge paradigm faces significant challenges stemming from two prominent trends: the rapid proliferation of sensor and user-generated data, and the real-time demands posed by modern applications, including augmented and virtual reality \[[38](#ch13.xhtml#r343)\].

The current centralized cloud architecture has demonstrated shortcomings in meeting the performance and scalability requirements associated with such data-intensive and real-time applications and services \[[38](#ch13.xhtml#r343),[39](#ch13.xhtml#r344)\]. This realization has sparked a growing interest in establishing an intelligent edge positioned between end-user/IoT devices and the cloud architecture. This intelligent edge is envisioned to address the evolving needs of applications and services, especially in the context of burgeoning data volumes and the heightened demands for real-time processing and responsiveness. ML plays a dual role in optimizing the operation of edge and fog systems and providing services for mobile applications. At the fog level, ML functions as a tool for optimizing the placement of various application functionalities, making decisions on how to distribute computational tasks across local, edge, and cloud/data center levels. Examples of fog-level optimization include determining the partitioning of computational tasks. On the edge level, ML is employed for tasks such as predicting changes in workload, orchestrating and initiating edge computing resources, and making decisions on application offloading, considering factors like workload on edge nodes and user mobility across the edge platform.

[Figure 5.3](#ch13.xhtml#f15) visually represents the twofold role of ML in optimizing the operation of edge/fog systems and delivering services for mobile applications.

![Figure 5.3 ML and Edge for service and infrastructure optimization.](html/images/9781032751245_C005_003.jpg)

Figure 5.3 ML and Edge for service and infrastructure optimization.

In its second role, ML takes input from the underlying edge/fog platform to enhance its operation. Training ML at the edge over wireless networks, while considering factors like latency and reliability, presents new research directions. ML optimization, based on behavioral and workload information from the edge/fog platform, offers advantages such as the ability to predict changes in workload concerning spatial and temporal constraints and user mobility. This enables real-time orchestration of edge resources and task offloading based on dynamic conditions.

### 5.5.1 ML-Based Optimization of Cloud/Fog Services

In Aryal and Altmann \[[40](#ch13.xhtml#r345)\], an intelligent resource allocation algorithm leveraging genetic algorithms is introduced for the optimal distribution of edge and cloud resources. Through an iterative process, the proposed algorithm aims to provide an optimal solution by aligning cloud provider resources with specific application requirements. The evaluations suggest that the algorithm is effective in achieving a Pareto-optimal match of cloud provider resources, and it can be customized to meet the specific needs of applications. The authors highlight future directions, emphasizing the importance of provider resource ranking and predicting application user behavior. However, challenges lie in the optimized utilization of resources with multiple objectives for application providers and the federation operator. The unique optimization objectives of each application, coupled with the heterogeneity of resources, contribute to the complexity of achieving optimal resource allocation for multiple applications.

In Calo et al. \[[41](#ch13.xhtml#r346)\], an architecture is presented for composing analytics functionality in the cloud and deploying them for use at the edge. This architecture facilitates the controlled distribution of data stream processing, with intensive yet well-structured processing occurring at the edge. The authors identify the main benefits of edge computing (EC) as reducing latency, minimizing data movement, and preserving local data privacy. The functionality split between observer and controller components is explored, and the supporting system tools required for efficiently composing distributed collaborative analysis applications for streaming data are identified. The overarching goal is to enable support for collaborative autonomous systems, primarily through the application of generative policy models.

### 5.5.2 ML-Based Optimization of the Edge Infrastructure

Numerous proposals and approaches address tasks and resource optimization in MEC platforms and MEC-based networks. In Wang et al. \[[42](#ch13.xhtml#r347)\], a multi-stack reinforcement learning algorithm is introduced for MEC to optimize tasks or resource allocations for users. The proposed algorithm enhances convergence speed and learning efficiency. Rodrigues et al. \[[43](#ch13.xhtml#r348)\] delve into efficiently utilizing MEC resources amidst a growing number of complex devices and services with an increasing number of configuration parameters, leveraging ML. The work in Rodrigues et al. \[[43](#ch13.xhtml#r348)\] also identifies computation and communication challenges related to MEC that can be addressed by various ML solutions.

Wu et al. \[[44](#ch13.xhtml#r349)\] propose and evaluate collaboration between edge and centralized cloud resources using ML, while Nduwayezu et al. \[[45](#ch13.xhtml#r350)\] employ deep reinforcement learning (DRL) to enhance online computing offloading for non-orthogonal multiple access.

Several proposals focus on specific technologies within MEC through ML interventions. For instance, Cao et al. \[[46](#ch13.xhtml#r351)\] and Cui et al. \[[47](#ch13.xhtml#r352)\] use multi-agent RL for cooperative caching and task offloading in MEC, respectively. Few works contributed to DRL-based energy-efficient task offloading for machine-type communication in the edge. Furthermore, some address minimizing per-bit energy consumption in 5G MECs using DNNs, improving user energy efficiency by developing a digital twin of the real system and training the DL system within it. Similarly, Cui et al. \[[48](#ch13.xhtml#r353)\] propose stochastic online learning for MEC, aiming to minimize the time-averaged operational costs of MECs. Subramanya et al. \[[49](#ch13.xhtml#r354)\] focus on ML-based service function chain placement and scaling in MEC, while still there is the need for AI or ML-based offloading in MEC and review state-of-the-art research in this direction.

### 5.5.3 Edge-Based ML Optimization

Federated learning (FL) emerges as a compelling approach for edge ML training architecture, as discussed in Chen et al. \[[50](#ch13.xhtml#r355)\] and Shlezinger et al. \[[51](#ch13.xhtml#r356)\]. FL allows computing nodes to collaboratively learn a shared prediction model while keeping training data decentralized on individual devices. In McMahan et al. \[[52](#ch13.xhtml#r357)\], a method for FL of deep networks based on iterative model averaging is presented, demonstrating robustness to unbalanced and non-identically distributed (non-IID) data distributions. Communication costs are recognized as a primary constraint, but the experiments reveal that FL can be made feasible in constrained environments, training high-quality models with relatively few communication rounds compared to synchronized stochastic gradient descent. The authors emphasize the practical privacy benefits of FL, including stronger guarantees via differential privacy, secure multi-party computation, and their combination. They view FL as a promising avenue for future research.

Chen et al. \[[50](#ch13.xhtml#r355)\] introduce a framework for implementing FL algorithms in wireless networks, optimizing collaboration between cellular base stations and wireless users to address challenges inherent in the wireless nature of the network. Convergence time optimization in FL is explored in Chen et al. \[[53](#ch13.xhtml#r358)\] and \[[54](#ch13.xhtml#r359)\], with a focus on minimizing convergence time while enhancing FL performance in resource-constrained cellular wireless environments.

In Konecný et al. \[[55](#ch13.xhtml#r360)\], FL is studied for optimizing ML prediction models using local training data. The authors propose a new algorithm for sparse convex problems, demonstrating the possibility of designing efficient algorithms utilizing local training data without leaving the source node at any phase. The work sets the stage for future research directions, including the development of a fully asynchronous version of the proposed algorithm and a deeper theoretical understanding.

Additionally, Lim et al. \[[56](#ch13.xhtml#r361)\] present a survey on using FL in MEC platforms. The article explores the background of FL in MEC, implementation challenges, and solutions and outlines future research directions in this evolving field.

## 5.6 Future Research Directions

Here, we discuss a few important research areas that require significant contributions for resolving some real problems the industry is still facing.

1.  **Future of ML in Physical Layer:** The application of ML techniques holds significant promise for enhancing the physical layer. Nevertheless, several inherent challenges need to be addressed. One such challenge involves the necessity of standardized benchmarks and datasets, which play a crucial role in assessing the performance of different ML algorithms. Despite this, there is currently a lack of comparable benchmarks or shared datasets specific to the physical layer. Further exploration is required to apply ML disciplines, particularly deep learning (DL), to the physical layer, addressing various perspectives. The efficacy of DL-based modulation recognition methods underscores their utility in feature extraction and recognition within this domain. Furthermore, integrating intelligence at the physical layer with higher layers of the network has the potential to create a versatile and intelligent overall network. The exploration of cross-layer intelligence sharing through ML techniques remains underexplored, presenting an intriguing avenue for future research.
2.  **Future of ML in Medium Access Control Layer:** While advancements have been achieved in proactive MAC algorithms by integrating sophisticated learning techniques, there remains a pressing need to explore and formulate more robust and stringent solutions to address challenges such as interference prediction, resource allocation, power optimization, and mobility prediction. Further research avenues include the convergence of licensed and unlicensed spectra, as well as leveraging machine learning techniques to enhance the efficiency of wireless networks by exploiting spectrum availability in both frequency bands.
3.   **Future of ML in Network Layer:** In addition to the separation of network control from the data forwarding plane, there are crucial considerations regarding the application of ML. Specifically, extensive research has explored the integration of ML into routing protocols for mobile ad-hoc networks (MANETs). Moreover, the security of these learning algorithms poses a significant challenge. In decentralized systems, the dissemination of misleading or incorrect information by sporadic devices can adversely impact the overall behavior of the network or routing protocols. Consequently, the utilization of ML in MANETs based on latency or time constraints and ensuring the security of the network present two compelling research challenges. The research challenges extend beyond MANETs, encompassing nearly all types of networks, where routing protocols face issues such as network overhead due to information retrieval for learning, latency in making learning-based decisions, and ensuring the security of the learning environment. Consequently, these challenges persist as compelling areas for further investigation in research.
4.  **Future of ML for Edge Computing:** Potential directions for future research encompass the advancement of methodologies leveraging ML as a pivotal element in decision-making processes for the allocation of application and service data and processing. This involves determining which components should be managed locally, which are at the edge, and which should be directed to data centers, known as ML for fog computing. These investigations should take into account factors such as performance, efficiency, and privacy considerations. Additionally, an intriguing avenue for future exploration lies in utilizing ML as a central component for orchestrating the management of edge infrastructure. This involves coordinating the allocation of nodes to serve users/end devices in specific logical or physical areas, or in certain application types. Another noteworthy perspective for future research revolves around the utilization of extensive datasets from diverse sources.

## 5.7 Conclusion

This chapter presents a comprehensive examination of the current state-of-the-art in the fields of ML disciplines, techniques, and tools as applied to communication networks. The discussion begins by elucidating the diverse applications of ML within wireless networks, spanning the physical, MAC, and network layers. Subsequently, this chapter explores ML applications in innovative technological paradigms such as SDN, NFV, and MEC. A specific emphasis is placed on delineating the role of ML in network security, underscoring its imperative for bolstering security operations in wireless networks. This chapter underscores the confluence of significant research endeavors that bridge ML disciplines with communication network technologies. Given the nascent deployment of ML in wireless networks, this chapter identifies numerous intriguing research questions warranting further investigation. Consequently, it outlines compelling future research directions spanning from the physical layer to the network layer and encompassing emerging networking technologies and services.

# chapter 6Cuckoo Search Optimizer Based on Aquila Exploration Strategy

Megha Varshney, Pravesh Kumar, Pinkey Chauhan, and Geeta Arora

DOI: [10.1201/9781003472506-6](http://doi.org/10.1201/9781003472506-6)

## 6.1 Introduction

The family of optimization algorithms known as metaheuristic algorithms, which draw their inspiration from natural events or processes, has attracted a lot of interest recently because of its capacity to effectively tackle challenging issues. Even in situations when conventional optimization approaches may be inefficient or unfeasible, these algorithms are made to search and explore enormous solution spaces in pursuit of optimal or nearly optimal solutions \[[1](#ch14.xhtml#r362)\].

Because they may be used in a variety of problem areas, including but not limited to engineering, finance, healthcare, logistics, and scheduling, metaheuristic algorithms are renowned for their adaptability and versatility. Genetic algorithms, particle swarm optimization, ant colony optimization, simulated annealing, and cuckoo search are a few well-known examples of metaheuristic algorithms.

The capacity of metaheuristic algorithms to quickly and roughly solve difficult problems is one of the key factors contributing to their popularity. In contrast to exact optimization approaches, which can demand time-consuming searches or difficult mathematical formulations, metaheuristic algorithms can produce near-optimal solutions rapidly and at a cheap computing cost. They are thus suited for real-world situations where it may be computationally impractical or time-consuming to determine the precise optimal solution.

The solution space can also be explored and used by metaheuristic algorithms in a non-deterministic, adaptive way. They have the ability to avoid local optima, which are poor solutions that trap conventional optimization techniques, and to modify their search strategies in response to the nature of the problem and the environment.

Additionally, because metaheuristic algorithms frequently offer many answers, decision-makers can select from a variety of possibilities that trade-off various objectives. This helps decision-makers in multi-objective optimization situations make more informed decisions by giving them flexibility and insight into the trade-offs between competing objectives.

Within the domain of metaheuristics, the cuckoo search (CS) algorithm stands out for its unique approach inspired by the brood parasitic behavior of certain cuckoo bird species \[[2](#ch14.xhtml#r363),[3](#ch14.xhtml#r364)\]. Introduced by Yang and Deb \[[3](#ch14.xhtml#r364)\], CS has gained significant traction due to its effectiveness in tackling continuous and even NP-hard (non-deterministic polynomial time) problems. The core principles of CS mimic the cuckoo’s strategy of laying eggs in other birds’ nests. In the context of optimization, each nest represents a potential solution, and the cuckoo eggs symbolize new solutions. The algorithm incorporates three idealized rules:

*   **Lévy Flight for Exploration:** Cuckoos employ a Lévy flight foraging strategy, characterized by long jumps interspersed with short exploratory movements. This mechanism allows CS to explore the search space effectively, avoiding premature convergence on local optima \[[4](#ch14.xhtml#r365)\].
*   **Discovery and Replacement:** A fraction of host birds (existing solutions) may discover the foreign cuckoo eggs (inferior solutions). These discovered eggs are then abandoned or replaced with new ones, promoting the survival of better solutions.
*   **Selection and Improvement:** The solutions (nests) with higher fitness values are carried over to the next generation, gradually leading to the identification of the optimal solution.

### 6.1.1 Evolution of CS and Its Limitations

Despite its success, CS has faced criticism for its susceptibility to premature convergence, particularly in complex problems with numerous local optima \[[5](#ch14.xhtml#r366),[6](#ch14.xhtml#r367)\]. Researchers have addressed this limitation by proposing various enhancements to the original CS framework. These improved variants incorporate diverse strategies like hybrid approaches with other metaheuristics, parameter tuning techniques, and adaptive search mechanisms \[[7](#ch14.xhtml#r368),[8](#ch14.xhtml#r369),[9](#ch14.xhtml#r370)\].

In 2021, Abualigah and Elaziz unveiled the revolutionary metaheuristic optimization technique known as the Aquila optimizer. It is intended to solve optimization problems effectively by emulating aquilas’ foraging techniques. The exploration capabilities of the algorithm are improved by the exploration method used by the Aquila optimizer, allowing it to seek for optimal or nearly optimal solutions in challenging problem domains. The method employs a series of operators, such as the capturing, exploring, and escape operators, inspired by the hunting behavior of Aquilas, to repeatedly update the solution space and enhance the quality of solutions across iterations. The Aquila optimizer has drawn interest as a potential metaheuristic algorithm in the field of optimization research because of evidence of its promising performance in a variety of optimization tasks.

Here are some potential benefits of using the Aquila optimizer exploration strategy in the CS algorithm:

*   **Enhanced Exploration:** The Aquila optimizer is known for its robust exploration capabilities. Its position update strategy, inspired by the hunting behavior of Aquila birds, allows for a wider search of the solution space. This can be particularly beneficial for CS, which can sometimes struggle with premature convergence, getting stuck in local optima. By incorporating Aquila’s exploration methods, the combined Aquila-cuckoo search (ACS) algorithm could explore a more diverse range of solutions, increasing the chances of finding the global optimum.
*   **Improved Search Diversity:** The Aquila optimizer utilizes various operators like “exploration” and “escape” that encourage movement away from potentially good solutions and toward unexplored areas. This can help ACS maintain a healthy level of diversity in the population of solutions, preventing them from clustering around local optima too quickly. A diverse population allows for a more thorough exploration of the search space.
*   **Reduced Premature Convergence:** As mentioned earlier, a major drawback of CS is its tendency to converge prematurely. By integrating Aquila’s exploration strategy, ACS can potentially avoid getting trapped in local optima. The exploration operators in the Aquila strategy can help the algorithm escape these areas and continue searching for better solutions throughout the optimization process.
*   **Potential for Faster Convergence:** While the primary focus of the Aquila strategy is exploration, it also incorporates elements of exploitation (focusing on promising areas). This combined approach might lead to a faster convergence on the optimal solution in ACS compared to the base CS algorithm. By efficiently exploring and then exploiting promising areas of the search space, ACS could find the optimal solution in fewer iterations.
*   **Adaptability to Complex Problems:** The exploration strengths of the Aquila strategy can be particularly valuable for tackling complex optimization problems with many local optima. By leveraging Aquila’s diverse search methods, ACS might be better equipped to navigate such challenging landscapes and locate the globally optimal solution.

It’s important to note that these are potential benefits, and the actual effectiveness of the Aquila strategy in CS will depend on the specific problem being addressed and the implementation details of the ACS algorithm. However, the theoretical advantages suggest that the combination of these two algorithms has the potential to be a powerful tool for optimization tasks.

The study is set up like this: The operation of the Aquila optimizer algorithm and cuckoo search is explained in Section 6.2. The proposed ACS is described in Section 6.3. Parameter configurations, simulation outcomes, and comparisons with other algorithms are covered in Section 6.4. The conclusion is found in Section 6.5.

## 6.2 Working on Basic Algorithms

This section explains the detailed functioning of the cuckoo search algorithm and the Aquila exploration strategy.

### 6.2.1 Cuckoo Search Algorithm

Levy’s flight-based compulsory parasitism of some cuckoo species serves as the foundation for the CS algorithm.

This is how the CS process is explained:

*   A cuckoo deposits an egg in a separate nest each time it does so.
*   The best nest that yields an egg is passed down to the following generation.
*   A limited quantity of host nests are accessible.

There is a likelihood *P*[*a*](0, 1) that host birds will find cuckoo eggs. The host bird in this scenario has two choices: it can either abandon the nest and build a new one or discard the eggs. In this instance, each egg can be considered a possible fix.

In the first stage of the CS process, when creating the *i*th solution in the (*k*+1)th generation, each solution is formed at random. The location of the nest or egg is updated by Eq. (6.1).

*x*[*i*](*k* + 1) = *x*[*i*](*k*) + *α* ⊗ *L*(*λ*)(6.1) 

where   
*x*[*i*](*k* + 1)  
 is the nest’s *i*th position of the (*k* + 1) generation in the population,   
*α*  
 is a real number, it marks the step size,   
⊗  
 represents entry-wise multiplications, and   
*L*(*λ*)  
 is the random search vector produced by Levy’s distribution.

### 6.2.2 Aquila Exploration Strategy

There exist two methods for the exploration of Aquila \[[10](#ch14.xhtml#r371)\].

**Exploration 6.1:** Expanded Exploration (*X*[1])

This method involves the Aquila flying over the search region at a higher altitude, exploring a large area, and then performing a vertical dive to find the prey once the target location has been identified. The following is how this behavior can be stated mathematically: $X\_{1}\\left( {k + 1} \\right) = X\_{\\text{best}}\\left( k \\right)\\left( {1 - \\frac{k}{T}} \\right) + \\left( {X\_{M}\\left( k \\right) - X\_{\\text{best}}\\left( k \\right) \\ast {rand}} \\right)$(6.2)

$X\_{M}\\left( k \\right) = \\frac{1}{N}{\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{N}{X\_{i}\\left( k \\right)}},\\quad\\forall i = 1,2,\\ldots,Dim$(6.3) 

In this equation, *X*[best](*g*) stands for the best position attained up until the current iteration, *X*[*M*](*g*) stands for the average position of all Aquilas in the current iteration *g*, and *g*[max] stands for the maximum number of iterations. *N* denotes the size of the population, while *r* is a chance number between 0 and 1.

**Exploration 6.2:** Narrowed Exploration (*X*[2])

Aquilas frequently use this hunting technique in which they swoop down in the intended region while using brief gliding, circle the prey, and then attack. The position update mathematical formula is written as:

  
*X*[2](*k*+1) = *X*[best](*k*) × *L**e**v**y*(*λ*) + *X*[*R*](*k*) + (*y*−*x*) × *r**a**n**d*  
(6.4) 

where *X*[*R*](*g*) stands for an Aquila location that is created at random, lambda stands for the dimension size, and rand is a random number between 0 and 1. The Levy flight, which has the following mathematical representation, is represented by the Levy(*λ*) function: ${Levy}\\left( \\lambda \\right) = s \\times \\frac{u \\times \\sigma}{\\left| v \\right|^{\\frac{1}{\\beta}}}$(6.5)

$\\sigma = \\left( \\left( ,\\frac{\\Gamma\\left( \\left( ,{1 + \\beta}, \\right) \\right) \\times \\sin\\left( \\left( ,\\frac{\\pi\\,\\beta}{2}, \\right) \\right)}{\\Gamma\\left( \\left( ,\\frac{1 + \\beta}{2}, \\right) \\right) \\times \\beta \\times 2^{({(,\\frac{\\beta - 1}{2},)})}}, \\right) \\right)$(6.6) 

*u* and *v* are random numbers ranging from 0 to 1, and *s*and *β* are fixed constant values with 0.01 and 1.5, respectively. During the search, the variables *y* and *x* are used to indicate the spiral shape, and the following equations provide their calculation:

  
$$\\left( \\left( ,\\begin{array}{l} {x = r\\sin\\theta} \\\\ {y = r\\cos\\theta} \\\\ {r = r\_{1} + U \\times D\_{1}} \\\\ {\\theta = - \\omega \\times D\_{1} + \\theta\_{1}} \\\\ {\\theta\_{1} = 3\\,\\pi/2} \\\\ \\end{array}, \\right) \\right)$$  
(6.7) 

where *r*[1] represents the number of search cycles ranging from 1 to 20, *D*[1] comprises integer numbers from 1 to the dimension size (  
*λ*  
), and *ω* is set to 0.005.

## 6.3 Proposed ACS Algorithm

In accordance with the previously provided definition, we put forth our algorithm, known as ACS \[[11](#ch14.xhtml#r372)\]. The two methods were modified as follows:

This program aims to identify the best solution for a given problem by iteratively updating a population of cuckoo eggs using the CS optimization algorithm and the Aquila exploration strategy. In order to determine whether cuckoo eggs are in an Aquila’s catch range or escape range, the bird will adjust its position accordingly. Levy flying is another technique that the algorithm uses to produce fresh cuckoo eggs. The term “capture range” describes the area where an Aquila can detect and capture a cuckoo egg while it is in the exploring phase. If an Aquila is detected to be in range to grab a cuckoo egg, Eq. (6.2) is applied to update the cuckoo egg’s position in relation to the direction and speed of the Aquila. The term “escape range,” on the other hand, describes the area beyond which an Aquila is unlikely to accidentally trap a cuckoo egg. A cuckoo egg’s position is updated using an escaping operator if it is discovered to be within Aquila’s escape zone by using Eq. (6.4). The CS algorithm’s Aquila exploration method takes into account both capture and escape range when considering how to update cuckoo eggs based on their proximity to an Aquila. The pseudocode of the proposed algorithm is given in **Algorithm 6.1**.

**Core Principles:**

*   **Leveraging Cuckoo Search (CS):** ACS builds upon the foundation of the CS algorithm. It utilizes an initial population of solutions represented as “cuckoo eggs.” These eggs are iteratively updated through the optimization process to find the solution with the best fitness value (optimal solution).
*   **Enhancing Exploration with Aquila Strategy:** The key innovation lies in integrating the Aquila exploration strategy into CS. This strategy, inspired by the hunting behavior of Aquila birds, injects robust exploration mechanisms into the algorithm.

**Exploration and Update Mechanisms:**

*   **Capture Range and Eq. (6.2):** The “capture range” defines the area where an Aquila bird can detect and potentially capture a cuckoo egg during its exploration phase. If a cuckoo egg falls within this capture range, Eq. (6.2) (not provided in the excerpt) is applied. This equation likely updates the cuckoo egg’s position based on the Aquila’s movement (direction and speed). This mechanism effectively guides the cuckoo egg toward potentially promising areas identified by the Aquila’s exploration.
*   **Escape Range and Eq. (6.4):** The “escape range” represents the zone beyond which an Aquila is unlikely to capture a cuckoo egg. If a cuckoo egg is detected within this escape range, Eq. (6.4) (not provided) comes into play. This equation likely involves an “escape operator” that updates the cuckoo egg’s position away from the Aquila. This mechanism prevents good solutions (cuckoo eggs) from being prematurely discarded due to Aquila’s presence.
*   **Random Walk:** In scenarios where the cuckoo egg is neither within the capture nor escape range of an Aquila, the pseudocode suggests a random walk operator with probability pd. This mechanism ensures continued exploration of the search space even in the absence of direct Aquila influence.

**Algorithm Procedure:**

1.  **Initialization:** The algorithm begins by receiving problem parameters, population size, maximum iterations, a parameter pa (likely controlling the replacement probability), and parameters specific to the Aquila exploration method. An initial population of cuckoo eggs (potential solutions) is then created.
2.  **Iteration Loop:** The algorithm enters a loop that continues for a predefined number of iterations. Within each iteration: 
    *   **Fitness Evaluation:** The fitness of each cuckoo egg (solution) in the population is evaluated.
    *   **Aquila Exploration and Update:** For each cuckoo egg: 
        *   The Aquila exploration strategy is applied. The egg’s position is potentially updated based on its proximity to an Aquila (using capture range, Eq. (6.2), escape range, and Eq. (6.4)).
    *    **Lévy Flight for New Eggs:** A new cuckoo egg (potential solution) is generated using the Lévy flight technique, a mechanism from the original CS algorithm known for its ability to promote exploration over large distances.
    *   **Fitness Evaluation and Replacement:** The fitness of the newly generated egg is evaluated. With a certain probability (p[a]), the new egg replaces the existing cuckoo egg if it has a better fitness value.
3.  **Selection and Update:** After processing all eggs in the population, they are likely sorted based on their fitness values. The iteration counter is incremented.
4.  **Termination:** The loop continues until the maximum number of iterations is reached.

Overall, the ACS algorithm merges the strengths of CS (exploration and exploitation) with the powerful exploration capabilities of the Aquila optimizer. By strategically updating cuckoo egg positions based on Aquila’s exploration strategy, ACS aims to achieve a more thorough exploration of the search space and prevent premature convergence on local optima.

The algorithms mentioned above are all minimizations. From F1 to F4, functions are unimodal and scalable; from F5 to F9, functions are multimodal, and fixed-dimensional multimodal F10.

### 6.4.1 ACS’s Intensification Capability

The results from [Table 6.3](#ch14.xhtml#t10) show the comparison of results between the basic algorithms AO and CS with ACS. And the obtained results show that ACS performs better than the original CS. Therefore, the addition of Aquila’s exploration capability with cuckoo search gives excellent results.To evaluate the ability of ACS to utilize global solutions, we used a set of unimodal functions (F1–F4), which possess only one global optimum. The results obtained from assessing the unimodal benchmarks for ACS and other existing competitors are presented in [Table 6.4](#ch14.xhtml#t11) (F1–F4), including the worst and best solutions found so far, as well as the average and standard deviation values from several runs. Our findings support the superiority of ACS over its competitors, as it outperforms them in achieving minimum values for the best and worst solutions found so far, as well as average values with high reliability and consistency (minimum STD) for six of the set’s functions. However, ACS provides the least optimal number for F7. This insight reveals ACS’s remarkable skill in enhancing the best solutions. The proposed ACS algorithm employs two exploitation strategies, namely expanded exploitation and narrowed exploitation, which primarily focus on the local search area. These strategies operate according to their fundamental procedures and facilitate the optimization process by explicitly and broadly targeting the neighborhood in the search.

### 6.4.2 ACS’s Diversification Capability

Analyzing the numerous local optima of multimodal benchmarks allows one to assess the ability of metaheuristics (MHs) to explore problem spaces. In our study, we assessed the performance of ACS using both multidimensional (F5–F9) and fixed-dimensional (F10) multimodal test functions. The study findings for the multidimensional (F5–F9) and fixed-dimensional (F10) functions, as well as the worst, average, best, and standard deviation values of the functions analyzed, were provided in [Table 6.4](#ch14.xhtml#t11). Our investigation revealed that ACS outperformed its rivals on the majority of the high-dimensional multimodal benchmarks. In fact, for five of the six functions in this collection, ACS supplied the worst, average, best, and STD values. As seen in [Table 6.4](#ch14.xhtml#t11), the SMA was the sole substantial rival for ACS. Our findings show that generally, ACS outperformed its rivals. Due to its two exploration tactics, which concentrate on the worldwide search area, ACS has performed exceptionally well. These tactics enable ACS to explore the search space accurately and efficiently, outperforming more recently demonstrated algorithms. The process of optimization is managed in two distinct ways when searching for a large search region: widely and narrowly.

### 6.4.3 Analysis of the Qualitative Convergence of ACS

To validate the effectiveness of the ACS model, we utilized the convergence curves of standard functions such as F1, F2, and F10, as illustrated in [Figure 6.1](#ch14.xhtml#f16).

![Figure 6.1 Convergence curve and parameter space of the functions F1, F2, and F12.](html/images/9781032751245_C006_001.jpg)

Figure 6.1 Convergence curve and parameter space of the functions F1, F2, and F12.

The second column of [Figure 6.1](#ch14.xhtml#f16) presents the convergence curve while the first column displays the function’s 2D shape indicating qualitative measurements. The convergence curve depicted in the last column of [Figure 6.1](#ch14.xhtml#f16) shows various patterns associated with the evaluated functions. For instance, unimodal functions that meet arcs are aspherical and require minor adjustments for better results. On the other hand, multimodal functions are more intricate and their convergence trajectory exhibits a stepwise pattern.

## 6.5 Conclusion

The study proposes a new hybrid optimization algorithm, ACS, which tackles a major limitation of CS – getting stuck on local solutions. ACS incorporates the exploration strengths of the Aquila optimizer into the CS framework. This allows ACS to effectively explore the search space and avoid premature convergence. This chapter showcases ACS’s superiority. It will have the potential application in various domains like big data, text mining, image processing, and even neural network training. ACS will emerge as a significant advancement in optimization, offering a robust tool for real-world problems across diverse disciplines.
